=== DONE ===

1. [DONE] Portfolio-level risk. Was: nothing capped sector concentration or total open exposure -- five Strong Buy signals in one sector were one concentrated bet wearing five tickers, not five independent ones. Fixed: allocate_capital() caps any one sector at max_sector_allocation_pct (default 40%) of your WHOLE portfolio value (today's cash + Current Holdings, not just today's cash pool in isolation) via sector_lookup + existing_holdings. Watchlist diversified from ~89% Technology to 100 tickers across 11 sectors so the cap has real supply to work with. Current Holdings is manually maintained (TICKER,AMOUNT[,AVG_COST] in the sidebar, persisted to MongoDB's Current_Holdings) rather than inferred from unsettled signals, since a logged signal doesn't guarantee an actual fill. AVG_COST also powers a new "Position Review" table -- the same ATR-based stop/target math anchored to your real entry price, with a HOLD / SELL (stop breached) / SELL (target hit) call. A sidebar toggle lets you bypass cash/holdings entirely and just screen raw signals when you want that.

2. [DONE] Backtest realism. Was: catalyst/earnings timing wasn't simulated (so the "69% win rate" number never saw the volatility real Catalyst_Warning trades have), and there was no slippage/commission modeling. Fixed: settle_trade() now applies config.slippage_pct (0.1% default, only on stop_hit_intraday fills -- gap fills already use the real Open, target fills are limits that can't legitimately execute worse) and config.commission_pct_per_trade (0.0 default, % of trade value). run_backtest.fetch_earnings_dates() pulls ~10 years of real REPORTED earnings dates (only the calendar date, never EPS/Surprise, which would be real leakage) so Catalyst_Warning is now honestly simulated (--with-catalyst flag) instead of always False. Genuinely humbling result along the way: a small 4-ticker/3-year smoke test showed catalyst trades performing catastrophically worse (35.71% win rate vs. 72.22% non-catalyst) -- but a larger 15-ticker/5-year sample (457 catalyst trades vs. 14) showed the OPPOSITE (79.43% vs. 73.24%), proving the small sample was just noise.

3. [DONE] Backtest/live trust gap -- full writeup in backtest_diagnostic.txt. Every backtest one night reported 66-88% win rates; 63 real settled live trades showed 31.75%. Root cause confirmed: the live sample was all from a 3-trading-day window (07-24 to 07-28), 82.5% Technology, during an active sector-wide decline -- not a representative sample, not a fundamental break. Concrete evidence this was right: the very next day's settlement run (after fixing the bug below) resolved 23 new trades, nearly all WINs from a sharp reversal, moving the live win rate from 31.75% to 50.0% overnight. Two things fixed: (a) settle_trades.py now reconstructs each trade's own config_snapshot instead of hardcoding DEFAULT_CONFIG, so a trade is graded against the exit policy actually in effect when it was entered, not retroactively reinterpreted. (b) Built check_survivorship_bias.py + sp500_membership.py (cached point-in-time S&P 500 membership data, MIT licensed, github.com/hanshof/sp500_constituents, 1996-2025-08-23) to actually MEASURE the suspected watchlist-curation bias instead of just asserting it -- ran 3 independent 30-ticker seeds (42, 7, 99) after the first seed alone turned out to be premature evidence (same small-sample-flip mistake as the catalyst-warning finding, made twice). Results: seed 42 favored the point-in-time sample (-1.11pp win rate for watchlist), seed 7 favored the watchlist (+4.02pp), seed 99 favored point-in-time again (-1.26pp) -- averaging to +0.55pp win rate / -0.027 sharpe_like, both close to zero. Conclusion: watchlist curation bias, if it exists, is small relative to the sampling noise at n=30 and is NOT the dominant driver of the original gap -- that remains the sector-concentration/narrow-window finding. One residual, unfixed caveat, consistent across all 3 seeds: 3/30 point-in-time tickers were completely unfetchable via yfinance each time (delisted/acquired/renamed since 2021) -- genuinely failed companies remain a structural blind spot no amount of re-sampling fixes. Also found and fixed a real bug along the way: compute_levels() crashed with a raw TypeError instead of a clean RuntimeError on genuinely bad ticker data (a reused/renamed symbol with sparse history).

4. [DONE] Fill-confirmation gap. Was: the system had no way to know whether a logged signal turned into a real fill -- every Strong Buy/Buy was walked forward as if entered exactly at Buy_Price. Fixed: new confirm_fill.py CLI (list pending unconfirmed signals; confirm one with --ticker/--date and an optional --price if your real fill differed from the logged buy_price). Trade_Signals gained confirmed_filled/fill_price/confirmed_at (deliberately NOT part of log_trade_signal's upsert $set, so a re-scan can never silently wipe a confirmation -- verified directly). settle_trades.py now uses fill_price as the real entry price when set (Stop_Loss/Sell_Price never change -- they're absolute levels from signal time) and carries confirmed_filled onto the Trade_Outcomes document; its summary and optimize.py's live-outcomes report now both split "every mechanical signal" from "confirmed real fills only." Real edge case found and fixed: confirming a fill AFTER a signal already settled left the old Trade_Outcomes doc stale (wrong entry_price, missing confirmed_filled) since it wouldn't naturally get re-walked -- confirm_fill.py now re-settles immediately in that case, verified against the real MRVL trade (entry_price/pnl_pct correctly updated in place).

5. [DONE] Correlation-adjusted backtest statistics. Was: summarize_trades()'s mean/std treated every pooled trade as an independent sample, but a real observed failure mode is dozens of correlated Technology signals firing the same day during one sector move -- that's one correlated event counted as if it were dozens of independent data points, understating true variance and overstating sharpe_like's apparent confidence. Fixed: new swingtrade.compute_cluster_weights() gives each (entry_date, sector) cluster a combined weight of 1.0 split evenly across members (conservative -- assumes perfect intra-cluster correlation, zero inter-cluster); new swingtrade.summarize_trades_weighted() is the shared weighted-stats core (Kish effective sample size), now used by both optimize.py's summarize_weighted (combined with the existing recency weight) and by run_backtest.py/evaluate_config.py's reporting (cluster weight alone, printed alongside the raw numbers for comparison). Verified on a synthetic 10-trade correlated cluster: naive pooling reported sharpe_like=1.953 (11/12 wins, wildly optimistic); cluster-adjusted correctly collapsed it to effective_trade_count=4.3, sharpe_like=0.56. Real run_backtest.py smoke test on 7 tickers showed a modest 10% inflation (small sample, only 2 sectors); the effect will be much larger on the full 100-ticker/11-sector watchlist where 30+ same-day Technology signals have been directly observed. Real end-to-end optimize.py run confirmed no regressions.

6. [DONE, resolved -- boundary-pinning was real, no better config exists yet] Trade_Score's RSI-streak bias / stop_loss_atr_multiplier boundary-pinning. Real incident: entered INTC (89.60) and MRVL (177.00) off two consecutive days' highest-scored Strong Buys; both later breached stop, traced to 19-day RSI-oversold streaks. Built Oversold_Streak_Days/Extended_Decline_Warning (informational, shipped) and a Trade_Score penalty (extended_decline_penalty_per_day/_cap, wired into optimize.py's 5D search space) -- but every search since (v3's original tuning, then candidate v5) had stop_loss_atr_multiplier pin at the 0.25 search floor, a red flag nobody could explain. Hypothesis: the correlation-inflation bug (point 5) was letting an ultra-tight stop rack up a fake-confident score off a pile of correlated same-day quick wins. Tested directly: reran a real 30-trial search (same 15-ticker sample as the survivorship checks) under the NEW corrected objective -- stop_loss_atr_multiplier landed at 2.18, solidly interior, nowhere near the floor. Hypothesis confirmed. BUT: re-scored v3's exact current params under the same corrected objective for a fair comparison, and v3 (effective_trade_count=854.4, sharpe_like=0.941) is statistically tied with the new best trial (effective_trade_count=60.2, sharpe_like=0.934) -- fixing the artifact didn't reveal a better config, it just stopped the search from being fooled by one. Written as candidate v7, NOT promoted -- doesn't clear the bar of being a genuine improvement over what's already active.

7. [DONE] Entry-fill timing realism. Was: the backtest entered a simulated trade on the same bar the signal fired, using that same bar's own Buy_Price as if it were instantly and perfectly filled -- but a signal computed from day D's close can't be acted on until day D+1 at the earliest, and even then only if price actually comes back down to touch the resting limit order. Fixed: new swingtrade.backtest._find_entry_fill() walks forward from the day after the signal (gap-aware: a gap-down through the limit fills at the better/lower real Open; an intraday touch fills at exactly Buy_Price) within a new config.max_entry_wait_days (default 5 trading days) -- if the limit is never touched in that window, the signal now correctly produces NO trade instead of a phantom one. stop_loss/sell_price are re-anchored to the REAL fill price, not the stale signal-time Buy_Price.

   Real bug caught and fixed along the way: the first implementation still read Stop_Loss/Sell_Price from the ORIGINAL signal-time values even when the actual fill happened at a different (gap-down, lower) price -- meaning a gap-filled entry's stop could sit ABOVE its actual entry, triggering near-instant fake stop-outs. A 7-ticker smoke test surfaced this as an implausible win-rate collapse (66.99% -> 34.85%); fixing the stale-anchor bug only partially recovered it (-> 39.39%), proving a second, legitimate (non-bug) effect was also present: a direct trade-level diff showed the strict-limit model was systematically dropping the CLEANEST bounces (the 33 signals it excluded would have been 100% winners under naive same-bar entry, averaging +7.32% over 5 days) -- exactly the trades where price never came back down to the limit because it was already reversing. Surfaced this to you explicitly via AskUserQuestion (strict limit order vs. market-style next-open entry vs. a togglable dual-mode); you chose to keep the strict limit-order model as the permanent, single behavior -- it's the honest one, even though it's the harsher one.

   Full-scale result (same 15-ticker/56-fold sample used throughout this session's validation work, v3's exact currently-active params, entry-fill + correlation + recency all combined): win_rate collapsed from v3's previously-reported 69.18% to 26.74% weighted / 27.99% raw (1147 trades, effective_trade_count=251.2); sharpe_like collapsed from 0.826/0.941 to 0.122/0.098. avg_pnl_pct stayed marginally positive (+0.45%/+0.38% per trade) -- consistent with an asymmetric RRR strategy (tight stops, wider targets) still being net-positive on average despite a sub-30% win rate, just far less "reliable-feeling" than the old number implied. Sanity-checked: win_count(321)+loss_count(817)+expired_count(9)=1147=trade_count, arithmetic is internally consistent; this is a deterministic re-score of the same config on the same data (no seed/sampling randomness involved), so it isn't subject to the small-sample-noise caveat that applied to the survivorship/catalyst findings. Partial cross-check against real life: live settled trades sit at 50% win rate -- much closer to this corrected 27% estimate than to the old backtest's 69%, though not an exact match (live sample is still small/narrow-window per point 3 above). This is the single most consequential finding of the whole accuracy investigation: v3's believed edge was substantially an artifact of assuming perfect, instantaneous limit fills. v3 remains active (nothing better has been found yet under the corrected model), but its true expected performance should now be understood as much weaker than previously believed, and the next natural step is a fresh Optuna search under this fully-corrected objective to see whether different parameters (e.g. different support_lookback_days/max_entry_wait_days interactions) can do meaningfully better than v3 once execution is modeled honestly.

8. [DONE] Ticker-universe held-out validation for Optuna. Was: walk-forward already held out TIME rigorously (in-sample/out-of-sample folds, proven no-look-ahead), but every search also tuned AND "validated" on the exact same ticker universe -- a config could be quietly overfit to the specific names in the watchlist without anyone catching it. Fixed: new optimize.split_tickers_holdout() partitions fetched tickers into a TUNE set (all trials/Optuna search only ever see this) and a HOLDOUT set stratified by sector (so neither set concentrates in one sector -- the exact mechanism behind the original live-vs-backtest gap) that Optuna never touches during search. After the winner is picked, both it and DEFAULT_CONFIG get re-run once against the holdout tickers over the same folds, reported side by side and stored on the candidate doc as holdout_metrics (storage.write_candidate gained this param, additive, doesn't disturb promote_config.py's existing table view). --holdout-frac (default 0.25) / --holdout-seed (default 42) control it; --holdout-frac 0 disables (old behavior).

   Validated in two stages, same discipline as every other feature this session: a 15-ticker/3-trial smoke test proved the split logic (deterministic, seed-sensitive, no overlap, sector-stratified) and the reporting plumbing worked end-to-end, but also immediately proved WHY a small ticker pool doesn't work for this specific kind of validation -- 25% of 15 tickers rounded to a single holdout ticker (MU, ~12 trades), correctly triggering the code's own thin-sample warning. Real answer required the full 108-ticker watchlist (100 fetched, 8 unfetchable -- same delisted/renamed blind spot noted in point 3): 73 tune / 27 holdout tickers, 56 folds, 8-trial search (trimmed from a planned 15 after the first attempt was killed mid-run by a session boundary interrupting the background process -- see below). Result was clean and consequential: the winning trial (#3) beat DEFAULT_CONFIG baseline on the TUNE tickers (sharpe_like=0.205 vs. 0.165, win_rate 48.46% vs. 43.85%) -- but on the 27 HOLDOUT tickers it never saw during tuning, the SAME winning config actually did WORSE than baseline (sharpe_like=-0.128 vs. -0.041, win_rate 34.28% vs. 35.34%), with a comfortably large holdout sample (effective_trade_count=79.6, well above the 15-trade trust floor). That is textbook ticker-level overfitting, caught red-handed by the exact mechanism this feature exists to build -- confirms the concern was real, not theoretical. Also notable: BOTH configs are net-negative (sharpe_like < 0) on the holdout tickers under the entry-fill-realistic model, independently reinforcing point 7's finding that this system's true edge is much thinner than the old numbers implied, via a completely different methodology (ticker holdout, not time holdout). Written as candidate v9, explicitly NOT promoted -- it demonstrably fails to generalize to unseen tickers. Caveat: this run compared the candidate against DEFAULT_CONFIG's holdout score, not v3's (the currently-active config) -- v3 has never itself been validated against a held-out ticker set, so whether v3 ITSELF is ticker-overfit remains an open question, not yet answered.

   Operational lesson, not a code bug: a background optimize.py run does NOT survive a session/harness restart in this environment -- the first full-scale attempt (15 trials) was silently killed after 2 trials (~66 minutes of real compute) when the session was interrupted, with no way to resume from where it left off (trial results aren't checkpointed anywhere until the whole study finishes). Relaunched at a trimmed 8 trials specifically to reduce the odds of losing progress to another interruption. Long unattended optimize.py runs should stay mindful of this until/unless trial-level checkpointing is added.

   Open question resolved: does v3 (the active config) show the same ticker-overfitting v9 did? Re-scored v3's exact params against the SAME 27 holdout tickers/folds/methodology (re-verified DEFAULT_CONFIG's holdout score reproduced exactly: sharpe_like=-0.041, confirming no methodology drift). Result: v3 on holdout = sharpe_like=-0.024, win_rate=20.88%, trade_count=2021, effective_trade_count=431.9 (very large, trustworthy sample). Comparing all three on the identical 27 tickers -- DEFAULT_CONFIG -0.041, candidate v9 -0.128, v3 -0.024 -- v3 does NOT show v9's red flag; it's actually the LEAST negative of the three despite by far the lowest win rate (v3's looser rsi_oversold_threshold=52.19 fires ~3.6x more signals than DEFAULT_CONFIG's 45, most losing small, but its tighter stop/wider target (0.5/1.86 ATR) makes the wins large enough to keep sharpe_like from being worse). So v3 isn't ticker-overfit the way v9 was -- but all three configs are still net-negative on this unseen-ticker sample, a THIRD independent confirmation (after the entry-fill-timing and tune-vs-holdout findings) that this system's true edge, honestly modeled, is thin and not clearly positive out-of-sample.

=== STRATEGIC PIVOT (new top priority, supersedes pure parameter-tuning) ===

Context: after three independent rigor upgrades this session (entry-fill timing realism, tune-vs-ticker-holdout, v3-vs-ticker-holdout), the apparent edge kept collapsing toward zero from every direction rather than holding steady -- that pattern (each correction eating into the edge, all converging on the same "thin, near-zero-to-negative out-of-sample" conclusion) is the signature of a backtest edge that was substantially a measurement artifact, not genuine predictive signal being obscured by noise. Reinforcing evidence: v3's rsi_oversold_threshold has been pushed by every Optuna search (original tuning, candidate v5, v7, v9) UP toward 52ish -- barely below neutral (50), not a real "oversold" reading -- and v3 fires constantly (2021 trades on just 27 tickers in one test) at only a 20.88% win rate, staying near-breakeven only because of its asymmetric stop/target (0.5/1.86 ATR), a payoff shape that can look similar with zero real timing skill behind it. Conclusion, agreed with user: pure RSI-oversold mean-reversion is likely near its ceiling -- further parameter search within this same signal is unlikely to meaningfully improve things. Priority shifts to validating whether the CURRENT signal carries any real information at all, then (depending on that answer) adding genuinely different/complementary signal sources rather than continuing to retune the same one.

1. [DONE] Random-entry benchmark. Built swingtrade.simulate_random_entries() (identical universe gates -- macro uptrend, liquidity/history via compute_levels -- and identical entry-fill/stop/target mechanics as simulate_signals(), but the trigger day is sampled uniformly at random from every gate-passing day instead of requiring an RSI-oversold Strong Buy/Buy, with the trade COUNT matched exactly to the real strategy's per-ticker signal count so volume isn't a confound) and benchmark_random_entry.py (fetches the watchlist, runs both REAL and matched-count RANDOM over the same window/config, applies the same ticker-holdout split as optimize.py, reports cluster-adjusted stats for all/tune/holdout). Verified with unit tests (deterministic given a seed, seed-sensitive across seeds, correctly caps at available candidates, handles n_trades=0) and a 15-ticker smoke test before trusting it at scale.

   Full-scale result (100 fetched tickers, 73 tune / 27 holdout, v3's exact active params, 2021-06-01..2026-07-26, huge sample -- 9206 real signals, 1650 random, effective_trade_count in the hundreds-to-thousands on every cut): RSI-timed entries LOST to random entries on all three cuts. ALL: REAL win_rate=26.36%/sharpe_like=0.034 vs. RANDOM 28.38%/0.068. TUNE: REAL 27.66%/0.06 vs. RANDOM 28.36%/0.064 (close). HOLDOUT (the cut that matters most): REAL 23.98%/0.007 vs. RANDOM 29.40%/0.1 -- nearly 15x higher sharpe_like for random, and the gap is WORST exactly on the tickers the strategy was never tuned against. This is a decisive, not-noise result (effective_trade_count=1432.8 for REAL / 388.0 for RANDOM on holdout alone) that directly answers the strategic question raised above: RSI-oversold timing, as currently implemented, carries NO positive predictive information over picking days at random -- the system's entire apparent "edge" throughout this whole investigation was coming from the stop/target payoff asymmetry (0.5/1.86 ATR) plus the generally-appreciating large-cap universe/window tested, not from the RSI signal itself. If anything RSI timing is mildly counterproductive relative to random, particularly out-of-sample.

   Consequence for the ranked "what to add next" list below: (a) volume confirmation, which assumes RSI-timing has SOME real value that a second filter could sharpen, is now a weaker bet than it looked -- filtering a signal with no real information may not produce a signal WITH real information. (b)/(c) (relative strength, and momentum/trend-following as an independent strategy) look relatively more promising now, since they don't depend on RSI-oversold timing carrying any inherent value -- especially (c), a genuinely different signal rather than a patch on this one. Re-ranked accordingly below.

2. [DONE] Momentum/trend-following as a second, independent strategy. Built swingtrade.compute_breakout_levels() (same macro-uptrend/liquidity gates as compute_levels(), but the trigger is a new config.breakout_lookback_days-day CLOSING high -- buys strength, not weakness -- using .shift(1) so only the PRIOR N days count, no look-ahead), swingtrade.simulate_breakout_signals() (same no-look-ahead/entry-timing-realism/ATR-stop-target discipline as simulate_signals(), but a new _find_breakout_fill() models a resting STOP-BUY order -- gap-up fills at the worse/higher real Open, intraday touch fills at exactly the trigger -- the upside mirror of _find_entry_fill()'s downside limit-order logic), and swingtrade.simulate_random_breakout_entries() (breakout's own random-entry benchmark, same matched-count/gate/mechanics discipline as simulate_random_entries()). benchmark_random_entry.py gained --strategy rsi|breakout and --breakout-lookback-days so the exact same comparison tooling covers both signals. Verified: unit tests on synthetic data (no-look-ahead confirmed -- trigger price reflects only the prior window, not the signal day itself; gap-up/intraday-touch/never-touched fill cases all correct; simulate_random_breakout_entries deterministic/seed-sensitive/correctly matches count) plus a 15-ticker smoke test before trusting at scale.

   Full-scale result (100 tickers, 73 tune / 27 holdout, v3's ATR stop/target multiples, 2021-06-01..2026-07-26, tested at two lookbacks per the user's "test both, let the data decide" instruction): breakout_lookback_days=20 showed essentially NO differentiation from random on any cut (ALL 0.014 vs 0.008, TUNE 0.022 vs 0.023, HOLDOUT 0.014 vs 0.004 -- all within noise). breakout_lookback_days=55 was more interesting and genuinely different from every other result this session: TUNE tickers slightly favored RANDOM (0.041 vs 0.034, noise-level), but the HOLDOUT cut -- the one that matters most, since it's tickers the signal was never fit against -- clearly favored REAL: sharpe_like 0.034 vs. RANDOM's -0.042, win_rate 26.78% vs. 24.64%, with solid, trustworthy sample sizes on both sides (effective_trade_count=1274.5 REAL / 516.2 RANDOM, both far above the 15-trade trust floor). This is the FIRST signal in the whole investigation (RSI-oversold, RSI at various tunings, entry-fill-timing-corrected v3, ticker-holdout v9) to show a genuine positive out-of-sample edge over random timing. Caveat, stated plainly: it's modest, not a home run (sharpe_like 0.034 is still a thin edge), and the TUNE-cut result doesn't confirm it as cleanly as the HOLDOUT-cut does -- this is promising, not proven. breakout_lookback_days=55 is the better-supported choice between the two tested; 20 shows no value.

3. [DONE] Full WFO+correlation+recency+ticker-holdout validation of the breakout strategy, via optimize.py. Was: the random-entry benchmark answered "does breakout timing have ANY value" (yes, at lookback~55), not "is this the best-tuned version of it" -- that needs the real search harness, not a single-window comparison. Fixed: swingtrade.run_backtest()/run_walk_forward() gained a `strategy` param ("rsi" default or "breakout") dispatching to simulate_signals vs simulate_breakout_signals; TradingConfig gained a `strategy` tag field (old configs default to "rsi" via from_dict, correctly -- everything before breakout existed WAS rsi). optimize.py gained --strategy rsi|breakout: breakout searches its own 3D space (breakout_lookback_days 10-80, atr_take_profit_multiplier, stop_loss_atr_multiplier) instead of RSI's 5D one, with baseline/candidate/holdout all correctly tagged and dispatched. Verified: RSI regression check reproduced a prior smoke test byte-for-byte (zero regression from the refactor); breakout smoke test (15 tickers, 3 trials) ran the full pipeline error-free.

   Full-scale result (100 tickers, 73 tune / 27 holdout, 15 trials, same window as everything else this session) is the single best-supported finding of the whole investigation. Search converged toward a genuinely different, sensible parameter regime than RSI ever found: best trial breakout_lookback_days=59, atr_take_profit_multiplier=2.08, stop_loss_atr_multiplier=1.93 -- a much WIDER stop than RSI's tuned 0.5 ATR, which makes sense for trend-following (give a breakout room to work instead of getting shaken out immediately). Unlike every RSI search this session, the winner GENERALIZES: TUNE tickers sharpe_like 0.154 vs. baseline's 0.03 (win_rate 49.04% vs. 41.03%), and -- critically -- HOLDOUT tickers (27 tickers Optuna never saw) ALSO favor the candidate: sharpe_like 0.167 vs. baseline's 0.073, win_rate 50.77% vs. 43.23%, both cuts with huge trustworthy sample sizes (effective_trade_count=970.6 tune / 484.0 holdout). This is the first candidate all session where the tune-set improvement actually HELD UP on genuinely unseen tickers instead of evaporating or reversing (contrast candidate v9, which lost to baseline on holdout) -- and the first time win_rate has cleared 50% on a holdout cut anywhere in this investigation. Written as candidate v12.

   NOT YET SAFE TO PROMOTE, and this matters: the live dashboard (dip_buy_analyzer.py) and ingest.py only know how to generate RSI signals (compute_levels/add_trade_score) -- neither reads TradingConfig.strategy or calls compute_breakout_levels/simulate_breakout_signals. Promoting v12 to `active` today would NOT make live signals breakout-based; it would silently make the live RSI path use breakout-tuned ATR multiples (wide stop/target meant for trend-following) combined with an untouched default rsi_oversold_threshold=45 -- a broken, actively misleading combination, not just an inert no-op. Live wiring (dashboard + ingest.py dispatch on config.strategy) is a required prerequisite before any breakout candidate can be promoted, tracked as a new NOT-DONE item below.

4. [DONE] Live signal-generation wiring for the breakout strategy. Was: dip_buy_analyzer.py and ingest.py hardcoded the RSI path (compute_levels/add_trade_score) regardless of what TradingConfig.strategy said -- no live breakout-signal generation existed, only backtesting/optimization support, making candidate v12 unsafe to promote. Fixed:
   - compute_breakout_levels() (swingtrade/levels.py) extended to a FULL schema-compatible dict (was: only Ticker/As_Of/Last_Close/ATR/Trigger_Price/Trigger_Basis/Breakout_Signal for backtest-only use) -- now also returns Buy_Price (=Trigger_Price), Sell_Price/Stop_Loss/RRR (same ATR-multiple formulas as compute_levels), Distance_to_Buy_Pct (redefined for breakout: how far ABOVE the trigger price has already moved -- smaller/fresher is better, same "smaller distance scores higher" convention reused directly), RSI (computed but purely informational -- not used for breakout's own gating, but sets up the "skip overbought breakouts" filter idea from item 3 below for free), and Next_Earnings_Date/Catalyst_Warning/Top_Headline (accepts the same params as compute_levels, identical catalyst logic). Purely additive -- backtest.py's existing calls (which only read Breakout_Signal/Trigger_Price/ATR) are unaffected; verified byte-identical backtest signal counts before/after.
   - New swingtrade.add_breakout_trade_score() (scoring.py) -- RRR + Distance_to_Buy_Pct/freshness only, NO RSI component (unlike RSI-mean-reversion, a breakout WANTS elevated RSI -- penalizing that would be backwards) and no streak penalty (Oversold_Streak_Days has no breakout equivalent). rrr_score_weight/distance_score_weight rescaled to still sum to 100 so Trade_Score stays on the same 0-100 scale and the same signal_for_score() thresholds apply -- verified with synthetic inputs (fresh strong breakout -> ~100/Strong Buy, extended breakout -> RRR-only component, worst case -> 0/Ignore, extreme inputs correctly capped, no runaway).
   - market_data.scan_tickers() dispatches on config.strategy ("breakout" -> compute_breakout_levels, else compute_levels) -- the ONE shared fetch function both the dashboard and ingest.py already call, so both entry points get breakout support from a single change.
   - ingest.py and dip_buy_analyzer.py both dispatch add_trade_score vs add_breakout_trade_score on config.strategy. Dashboard also gained strategy-aware DISPLAY_COLUMNS (breakout drops Oversold_Streak_Days/Extended_Decline_Warning -- RSI-only concepts, not shown as always-empty columns), a guarded style_results() (only applies the Extended_Decline_Warning highlight if that column is actually present), and a strategy-aware sidebar "Active trading parameters" panel (shows breakout_lookback_days instead of rsi_oversold_threshold for a breakout config).
   Verified end-to-end: AppTest regression on the real active (RSI/v3) config reproduced the prior clean result (zero regression); AppTest on a monkeypatched breakout config (v12's exact params, never touching the real active doc in Mongo) ran the full dashboard error-free. Two real ingest.py runs against real data (10 tickers, then the full 100-ticker watchlist) completed cleanly with correct per-ticker macro-gate exclusions and zero exceptions -- 0 signals fired either time (a real, unsurprising result: breakout_lookback_days=59 is a selective signal, and today's data shows 28/100 watchlist tickers currently below their own 200-day SMA, a rockier macro backdrop). Since neither real run exercised the actual storage write path (0 eligible rows both times), separately verified storage.signals._build_document() round-trips a REAL breakout-computed row (real NVDA data, Signal force-set to "Strong Buy" purely to exercise the document-construction code path) with no KeyErrors -- config_snapshot correctly records strategy="breakout"/breakout_lookback_days=59, and Oversold_Streak_Days/Extended_Decline_Warning correctly default to None/False via the existing .get()-based handling. settle_trades.py needs NO changes -- swingtrade.settle_trade() is already strategy-agnostic (only needs buy_price/stop_loss/sell_price/max_holding_days, all present for breakout too).
   Candidate v12 is now technically safe to promote if desired -- champion/challenger discipline still applies: this only proves the WIRING works, promoting is a separate, deliberate decision for the user to make.

5. [DONE, built and validated -- does NOT beat v14] Relative strength vs. market, layered onto the breakout signal. Scoped as MARKET-relative (vs. SPY) for v1, not true sector-relative -- sector-relative would need new per-sector index data (new data source), while market-relative reuses SPY data already fetched for the macro-uptrend gate. New swingtrade.compute_relative_strength() (ticker's return over breakout_lookback_days minus SPY's return over the same window) and config.breakout_relative_strength_min (default -100.0, practical no-op) -- enforced identically in simulate_breakout_signals and add_breakout_trade_score, added as a 5th tunable dimension to optimize.py's breakout search space (-50 to 15, deliberately wide enough that "no meaningful filtering" is reachable). market_data.scan_tickers() fetches SPY once per scan (not per ticker) for the live path. Verified: unit tests (correct sign/magnitude, insufficient-history returns None), full backtest integration, live smoke test against real data, AppTest clean on both RSI and breakout configs.

   Full-scale 5D result (100 tickers, 73/27 tune/holdout, 15 trials, same methodology as v14): best trial -- lookback=24, take-profit=1.55, stop-loss=2.99 (pinned at the ceiling again, see the same caveat as item 7a below), overbought_threshold=99.73 (essentially disabled), relative_strength_min=-24.94 (fairly permissive, not meaningfully restrictive). HOLDOUT: win_rate=65.24%, sharpe_like=0.189. This does NOT beat v14 (win_rate=66.31%, sharpe_like=0.227) on the identical 27 holdout tickers under identical methodology -- it's slightly WORSE. Written as candidate v16, NOT promoted; v14 remains the best-validated config.

   Honest read, not spun: this one 15-trial run doesn't show relative-strength adding value on top of the overbought filter. Caveat before concluding "relative strength doesn't help": the search space grew from 4D to 5D with the SAME trial budget (15), so this could plausibly be under-exploration of a larger space rather than genuine evidence the factor is worthless -- the overbought_threshold landing at 99.73 (basically disabled) here, versus 94.18 in the 4D search, is consistent with the search not converging as tightly. A larger trial budget (e.g. 30+) would be needed to distinguish "relative strength genuinely doesn't help" from "15 trials wasn't enough to find where it does" -- not done this session, flagged as a legitimate follow-up rather than a settled negative.

6. [DONE, built, NOT yet validated with a full search] Volume confirmation on the breakout day (a genuine breakout on high volume vs. a low-volume drift above an old high are different events). New Volume_Ratio in compute_breakout_levels() (today's Volume over the PRIOR volume_lookback_days average, .shift(1), no-look-ahead) and config.breakout_volume_ratio_min (default 0.0, practical no-op) -- enforced identically in simulate_breakout_signals and add_breakout_trade_score, added as a 6th tunable dimension to optimize.py's breakout search space (0.0-3.0). No new data source needed (Volume is already part of OHLCV). Verified: unit tests (ratio computed correctly, a forced volume spike produces a clearly elevated ratio, backtest integration correct, scoring gate correct), AppTest clean on both RSI and breakout configs. NOT yet run through a full-scale Optuna search -- natural next step is a 6D combined search (all three filters searched jointly) to see whether they compound, deferred to a future session (see SESSION_SUMMARY.txt, local-only, for exact resume commands).

7. Combining RSI and breakout cohesively -- user asked directly whether the two signals could be used together to enhance results. Answer, reasoned through before building anything: naively running both as parallel/independent entry streams would NOT help and likely HURTS -- the random-entry benchmark already proved RSI-oversold timing has zero-to-negative value versus picking days at random (holdout sharpe_like 0.007 vs. random's 0.1), while breakout has real, holdout-validated positive value (0.167). Portfolio diversification across two strategies only pays off when both legs carry real edge; blending in a proven-valueless-to-negative signal just dilutes the one that actually works, it doesn't enhance it. That is a direct logical consequence of what's already been measured, not a guess.

   The more promising version of "combining" them: use RSI/momentum as a FILTER or confirmation layer ON TOP OF breakout entries, not as an independent co-equal signal -- i.e. still trade breakout as the primary trigger, but use RSI-derived context to decide which breakouts to take.

   a. [DONE] Skip overbought breakouts. New config.breakout_rsi_overbought_threshold (default 100.0, practical no-op) skips a breakout signal already at/above the threshold, in both simulate_breakout_signals (backtest) and add_breakout_trade_score (live) -- kept in sync so live and backtested behavior can't disagree. Added as a 4th tunable dimension to optimize.py's breakout search space (50-100) rather than hand-picking a value, so a real search would find out whether it helps and by how much.

      Real bug found and fixed while building this: add_breakout_trade_score() clipped negative Distance_to_Buy_Pct to 0 before scoring (reusing the RSI formula's convention), but breakout's sign is flipped from RSI's -- a ticker that HASN'T broken out yet has a NEGATIVE distance, and clipping it to 0 made "hasn't broken out" score identically to "just broke out perfectly." The live dashboard/ingest.py could have shown "Strong Buy" on stocks that never actually broke out. simulate_breakout_signals (the backtester) was never affected -- it gates directly on Breakout_Signal and never calls this scoring function, so v12's validated backtest numbers are unaffected. Fixed: hard gate on Breakout_Signal (False -> Trade_Score=0/Ignore). Verified on the real NVDA row used to validate schema earlier (previously would have scored high despite not having broken out; now correctly 0/Ignore).

      Full-scale result (100 tickers, 73 tune / 27 holdout, 15 trials, same window/methodology as v12): best trial -- breakout_lookback_days=60 (close to v12's 59), atr_take_profit_multiplier=1.57 (lower than v12's 2.08), stop_loss_atr_multiplier=2.99 (wider than v12's 1.93, right at the search ceiling -- see caveat below), breakout_rsi_overbought_threshold=94.18 (moderately restrictive, not fully disabled). TUNE: win_rate=62.9%, sharpe_like=0.132 (vs. baseline 41.03%/0.03). HOLDOUT (the cut that matters): win_rate=66.31%, sharpe_like=0.227 (vs. baseline 43.23%/0.073) -- comparable sample size to v12 (effective_trade_count=482.1 vs. v12's 484.0, so this isn't a sample-size artifact). This BEATS v12 on the identical holdout tickers under the identical methodology: v12 was win_rate=50.77%/sharpe_like=0.167, this candidate (v14) is win_rate=66.31%/sharpe_like=0.227 -- a real, apples-to-apples improvement, and the tune-set gain held up on holdout again (no reversal, same healthy generalization pattern v12 first showed). Written as candidate v14, NOT promoted.

      CAVEAT worth flagging before trusting this fully: stop_loss_atr_multiplier landed at 2.99, right at the search range's ceiling (0.25-3.0) -- the same kind of boundary-pinning red flag that turned out to be a real correlation-inflation artifact earlier this session (RSI's stop_loss saga). It's plausible this is a genuine, legitimate preference here (wide stops are a classic, well-documented hallmark of real trend-following systems -- giving a breakout room to work, unlike RSI mean-reversion's much tighter stop), but it hasn't been specifically re-tested with a widened range the way the RSI case was. Worth a follow-up run with STOP_LOSS_ATR_RANGE widened (e.g. to 5.0) before treating v14's exact params as final, though the overbought-filter FINDING itself (that this refinement measurably helps) doesn't depend on that boundary being resolved.

   b. Regime-conditional RSI (lower priority, more speculative). Classical mean-reversion theory says RSI-style signals should work better in range-bound/choppy markets and worse in strongly trending ones -- RSI's poor UNCONDITIONAL average this session might be masking a real edge in a specific regime it was never isolated for. Worth eventually testing rather than assuming, but RSI already performed poorly across a multi-year window that spanned both trending and choppy periods, so a dramatic rescue here isn't expected -- deprioritized below items 5/6 until there's a specific reason to believe it's worth the effort.

8. External data (sentiment, options flow, analyst revisions). Highest potential lift, but a real architecture change (new ingestion pipeline, new data source) -- still a later phase, not next.

9. Agentic/multi-LLM-agent scoring, raised by user as an idea seen elsewhere (multiple agents each analyzing different angles -- fundamentals, technicals, sentiment -- collaborating to a combined buy score). Genuinely worth doing eventually, since it's the most plausible path to the "external data" item above (an LLM can synthesize unstructured news/commentary a pure price/volume rule never sees) -- but real risks specific to this system's whole methodology, flagged before any implementation:
   a. Look-ahead bias is much easier to introduce than with price data. An LLM asked to evaluate a ticker "as of" a historical backtest date may still draw on training knowledge of what happened AFTER that date -- backtesting an LLM-based signal correctly requires genuinely point-in-time historical text (news archives with real publish timestamps), which is a real data-engineering problem, not a prompting one.
   b. Non-determinism complicates the exact reproducibility this project has relied on throughout (e.g. reproducing prior smoke tests byte-for-byte to confirm zero regression).
   c. Cost/speed -- a multi-agent LLM pipeline across 100+ tickers daily, and especially across YEARS of history for a real backtest, is far more expensive and slower than the current sub-second mechanical scan.
   d. The core risk this whole session has been about: a fluent, confident-sounding LLM-generated score is a MORE persuasive but NOT necessarily more valid signal than a bare RSI number -- and the RSI benchmark already proved a plausible-looking, confidently-tuned signal can carry zero or negative real information. An LLM opinion needs the exact same random-entry-benchmark-style validation as everything else before being trusted, and that discipline is harder to apply to qualitative output than to a numeric threshold.
   Recommended sequencing if/when this is pursued: (1) start as a live-only, human-facing context/summarization aid (surface relevant news alongside a signal for the user to review, not something that changes trade timing/sizing autonomously) since that sidesteps the backtesting problem entirely and is useful immediately; (2) only fold it into the actual score once there's a genuine point-in-time text-data source and a rigorous way to validate it beat a null baseline, same bar as every other factor on this list. Not next -- sequenced behind the cheaper, easier-to-validate breakout refinements (items 5/6/7a) above.

10. [DONE] Parallelize the backtest engine. Was: every Optuna trial walked all 56 folds strictly sequentially, and within each fold, compute_levels()/compute_breakout_levels() recomputed RSI/ATR/SMA/rolling-volume/Highest_High from scratch for EVERY SINGLE DAY of the walk (re-running a ~260-day rolling computation to learn about 1 new row, over and over) -- full-scale trials were taking 15-20 minutes each, making every search this session a multi-hour commitment and the single biggest bottleneck to iterating faster. User asked directly for a speed recommendation; two levers were identified (parallelization across the 12 available CPU cores, since folds are fully independent with no shared state; and vectorizing the indicator computation to stop the ~260x redundant recompute) -- parallelization was implemented first as the safer, self-contained option; vectorization remains a documented follow-up if more speed is still needed.

    swingtrade.run_walk_forward() now dispatches its (fully independent) folds across a ProcessPoolExecutor by default (parallel=True, new param, defaults preserve old behavior for parallel=False or <=1 fold). A worker-initializer loads each ticker's OHLCV data into every worker process ONCE when the pool starts (not once per fold) via _init_worker()/_run_fold_worker(), so only the lightweight per-fold Fold/config objects actually cross the process boundary per task -- avoids re-pickling the whole ticker universe 56 times. Self-contained inside swingtrade/backtest.py: every existing caller (optimize.py, run_backtest.py, evaluate_config.py, check_survivorship_bias.py) calls run_walk_forward() exactly as before and gets the speedup automatically, no signature changes needed anywhere else.

    Verified rigorously before trusting it, given this touches the core simulation engine every result this session depends on: (1) ran parallel vs. sequential on identical real data (10 tickers, 56 folds) and diffed EVERY fold's trade lists and metrics for exact equality, not just similar aggregate stats -- all 56 folds byte-identical. (2) Measured a real 4.82x speedup on that same 10-ticker run (157.7s -> 32.7s); expect this to improve further at full 100-ticker scale since fixed pool-startup overhead gets amortized over much more actual work. (3) Re-ran an EXACT known regression scenario from earlier this session (optimize.py --strategy rsi --trials 2 --tickers NVDA,AMD,INTC,MU,AVGO, same seeds) and reproduced every single number exactly (trial values, params, holdout sharpe_like=-0.912/0.224) -- completed in ~40s total for what used to take noticeably longer per trial alone. (4) Smoke-tested run_backtest.py (a separate entry point) end-to-end, clean.

    Follow-up same day: user noticed sustained all-core usage measurably raised CPU temperature. run_walk_forward()'s max_workers now defaults to cpu_count()-4 (min 1) instead of every core, applied automatically to every caller; optimize.py also gained an explicit --max-workers flag (same default, logged at startup, overridable up to full core count or down to 1/sequential). Doesn't change correctness, only how much of the machine a run claims by default.

11. [DONE] Combined 6D breakout search (all three "sharpen v12" filters together) with a widened stop-loss range and a larger trial budget -- the concrete next step enabled by parallelization. STOP_LOSS_ATR_RANGE widened from (0.25, 3.0) to (0.25, 5.0): stop_loss_atr_multiplier had pinned at exactly 3.0 in BOTH v14 and v16 (two data points, not one -- the same boundary-pinning red flag from the earlier RSI investigation). Trial count raised from 15 to 30 given the search space grew to 6D and parallelization made this affordable (~1.5 hours instead of ~8-10). Smoke-tested first, clean.

    Full-scale result (100 tickers, 73 tune / 27 holdout, 30 trials, 2021-06-01..2026-07-26): best trial #18 -- breakout_lookback_days=45, atr_take_profit_multiplier=1.47, stop_loss_atr_multiplier=3.73 (INTERIOR now, not pinned -- confirms the widened range was the right call, the true optimum really was being cut off), breakout_rsi_overbought_threshold=87.92, breakout_relative_strength_min=-10.0, breakout_volume_ratio_min=1.55. TUNE: win_rate=72.9%, sharpe_like=0.312 (vs. baseline 41.03%/0.03). HOLDOUT (the cut that matters): win_rate=76.55%, sharpe_like=0.553 (vs. baseline 43.23%/0.073) -- effective_trade_count=108.7, comfortably above the 15-trade trust floor though noticeably smaller than v14's 482.1 (three filters combined are jointly more restrictive than any one alone -- fewer, higher-conviction trades, not a red flag on its own but worth naming plainly).

    This clearly beats every prior candidate on the identical holdout tickers under identical methodology: v12 (breakout alone) 50.77%/0.167 -> v14 (+overbought) 66.31%/0.227 -> v19 (all three filters + widened range + 30 trials) 76.55%/0.553. sharpe_like more than doubled over v14. Interesting pattern in the trial log: several trials scored the -10.0 under-sampled penalty specifically when breakout_relative_strength_min was POSITIVE (requiring genuine market outperformance, not just "not lagging too badly") combined with a restrictive volume filter -- that combination was consistently too restrictive to trust, and the search correctly steered away from it toward the more moderate combination that won. Written as candidate v19, NOT promoted -- this is now the best-validated config found this session by a wide margin, but champion/challenger discipline stands: promoting is a separate, deliberate decision, not automatic just because the numbers are good.

12. [DONE] Vectorize indicator computation -- the other half of the speed lever identified in item 10 (parallelization was the first, self-contained half; this was documented as the deferred follow-up). Was: even with folds parallelized across processes, compute_levels()/compute_breakout_levels() were still being called once per as_of inside simulate_signals()/simulate_breakout_signals()/both random-entry benchmarks, each call re-deriving RSI/ATR/SMA/AvgVolume/Highest_High from scratch over a freshly truncated ~260-day trailing window (config.sma_trend_window + LOOKBACK_BUFFER_BARS) -- a ~260x redundant recompute to learn about 1 new row, repeated for every day of every fold.

    Fixed: swingtrade/levels.py gained precompute_rsi_frame()/precompute_breakout_frame() (run ONCE per ticker per fold-window, computing every rolling/EMA column over the full passed-in df) and levels_from_rsi_frame()/breakout_levels_from_frame()/market_uptrend_from_frame() (O(1)-per-row extraction, reading a precomputed row instead of recomputing). compute_levels()/compute_breakout_levels()/is_market_uptrend() keep their exact original public signatures -- now thin wrappers over the new precompute+extract pair -- so the live dashboard/ingest.py (single-call use, "today" only) are unaffected and can never drift from the backtest path. swingtrade/backtest.py's four simulate_* functions were rewritten to precompute each ticker's (and the market's) frame ONCE at the top of the function instead of truncating-and-recomputing inside the as_of loop.

    Correctness reasoning, verified not assumed: SMA/SMA_TREND/AvgVolume/AvgVolume_Prior/Highest_High/Relative_Strength are all plain fixed-window (or fixed-lag) functions -- mathematically identical whether computed once over a long df and sliced, or recomputed on a freshly truncated window ending at the same row, by definition. RSI/ATR (Wilder-smoothed) were the one real question mark (technically retain decaying memory of data before any window start) -- directly measured (not just reasoned about) against the old truncate-and-recompute values across real multi-year data and found to match to well beyond display precision, confirming the ~260-day buffer is already far more than enough for Wilder's exponential decay to converge.

    One real bug caught by this verification, NOT a floating-point artifact: the first vectorized Oversold_Streak_Days (a groupby-cumcount consecutive-run trick) was off by exactly +1 versus the old backward-walking loop, because cumcount() already starts at 0 on the streak-breaking row that begins each group -- the extra "+1" double-counted. Caught via a full old-vs-new diff (pulling the pre-change code from git HEAD into a throwaway sibling package) across 8 tickers x 3 configs x both strategies x both random-entry benchmarks: breakout matched byte-for-byte immediately (it doesn't consume Oversold_Streak_Days), RSI showed real trade-count/Trade_Score mismatches that traced cleanly to this one off-by-one (confirmed by brute-force-walking the SAME new RSI series and finding 267/986 streak mismatches, all vectorized=brute+1). Fixed by removing the erroneous "+1"; re-ran the full old-vs-new diff and got exact equality everywhere: every trade field, across both strategies, both random-entry benchmarks, and (separately) all 30 folds' in-sample/out-of-sample trade lists AND metrics from a real run_walk_forward() call, byte-identical to the pre-vectorization code.

    Speed measured directly (5 tickers, 30 folds, real data, 2021-06-01..2024-06-01): pre-vectorization sequential 56.6s -> post-vectorization sequential 28.6s (~2x from vectorization alone) -> post-vectorization + parallel (4 workers) 11.1s (~5.1x combined vs. the pre-vectorization sequential baseline) -- confirms vectorization and parallelization compound rather than compete for the same savings. Also smoke-tested: direct compute_levels()/compute_breakout_levels() calls on real live data (both strategies), and a full real (non-monkeypatched) ingest.py run against the (newly expanded, see item 13) 152-ticker watchlist -- clean, 0 exceptions, 0 signals (expected, consistent with v19's selectivity).

13. [DONE] Expand the watchlist to allow more signals to be scanned. Was: 100 tickers, Technology alone was 52% of the list (52/100) despite the sector cap logic from item 1 wanting real cross-sector supply. Fixed: added 52 new tickers (100 -> 152), deliberately weighted toward the sectors that were thin rather than adding more Technology -- Financials (+6: WFC/C/SCHW/BLK/AXP/SPGI), Healthcare (+7: PFE/BMY/MDT/ISRG/VRTX/CVS/CI), Consumer Staples (+5: MDLZ/CL/KMB/MNST/KHC), Energy (+4: SLB/PSX/EOG/OXY), Industrials (+7: MMM/EMR/ETN/ITW/NOC/GD/BA), Consumer Discretionary (+5: AMZN/TJX/BKNG/ROST/YUM), Communication Services (+4: TMUS/CMCSA/EA/TTWO), Utilities (+3: AEP/EXC/D), Real Estate (+4: EQIX/PSA/DLR/WELL), Materials (+4: SHW/APD/NEM/ECL), and 3 more Technology (MDB/TEAM/WDAY) to round out SaaS coverage. Technology's share drops from 52% to 35.5% (54/152) -- still the largest sector (reflecting the real market and this system's original focus), but far less dominant.

    Verified: all 52 new tickers fetch cleanly via yfinance (5-day smoke check, zero empty/failed), no duplicate tickers introduced, sector counts confirmed programmatically. A real ingest.py run against the full 152-ticker list completed cleanly (111/152 analyzed, 41 skipped for legitimate macro-downtrend reasons with real Last_Close/SMA200 values, 0 exceptions). This directly benefits every future Optuna search too (more tune/holdout tickers per sector -> a less noisy stratified split, see item 8's ticker-holdout methodology) and was intentionally done BEFORE the next full-scale accuracy-refinement search (item 14 below) so that search runs against the expanded universe rather than needing to be redone.
