    Before concluding anything, re-scored v19's EXACT stored params against this SAME new 40-ticker holdout split and fold set (same discipline as every other candidate comparison this session -- never compare against a stale differently-computed number): win_rate=70.81%, sharpe_like=0.248, effective_trade_count=161.1 (both higher than v20's holdout numbers, and a LARGER effective sample too, so this isn't a small-sample fluke favoring v19). v19 clearly wins this apples-to-apples comparison -- v20 written as a new candidate (System_Config version=20) but NOT promoted, and per champion/challenger discipline nothing should be promoted here anyway since it's the worse-performing config.

    Interpretation: this is a genuinely useful negative result, not a wasted run. It's the first time v19's params have been tested against a holdout set drawn from a substantially larger, more diverse ticker universe than the one they were originally tuned on (100 tickers -> 152), and they held up well -- actually slightly BETTER than a config freshly tuned on the new universe with the same trial budget. Plausible reading: 30 trials may simply not be enough to reliably beat an already-good config when the search space is 6-dimensional and the ticker sample just got 52% bigger (more trials would explore more of the space, similar to the under-exploration caveat noted for v16 in item 5) -- this is evidence v19 is robust, not proof that no better config exists. v19 remains the active, best-validated config; no promotion action taken.

15. [DONE, step 1 of item 9's sequencing] Live-only AI news-context aid. Item 9 above recommended starting with "a live-only, human-facing context/summarization aid... since that sidesteps the backtesting problem entirely" before ever considering a scored, blended AI signal -- that step is now built. New ai_context.py: given a Strong Buy/Buy ticker's recent headlines, generates a short 2-3 sentence plain-language summary for a human to read. Deliberately narrow scope, matching item 9's own risk analysis: NEVER feeds into Trade_Score/Signal/position sizing, and is NEVER called from swingtrade/backtest.py -- sidesteps both the look-ahead-bias risk (an LLM's training knowledge leaking into a "historical" evaluation) and the "confident-sounding output isn't automatically valid" risk this whole session's RSI investigation already proved. Wired in as an opt-in, off-by-default feature: a sidebar checkbox in dip_buy_analyzer.py (disabled/grayed out if unavailable) and an ingest.py --with-ai-context flag. Degrades gracefully (returns None/False, never raises) if unconfigured.

    Uses Google Gemini's free tier (gemini-2.5-flash via the google-genai package, GEMINI_API_KEY env var) rather than a paid API -- a deliberate choice so this feature costs nothing to run, made after the user asked specifically for a free option. (Anthropic's API was the original prototype; swapped out before any real usage, so no cost was ever incurred.) Verified: graceful degradation with no key set (is_available()=False, summarize_ticker_context() returns None, no exceptions), and a full AppTest run of the live dashboard (152-ticker real scan, needed a 400s timeout not the default 120s) confirmed no exceptions and the new checkbox renders correctly. NOT yet verified against a real generated summary (no API key configured in the dev environment this was built in) -- the code path is verified correct and safe, but an actual Gemini response has not yet been observed; worth a real end-to-end check once a GEMINI_API_KEY is set.

    Step 2 (folding this into an actual independent, multi-agent SCORE -- separate agents for chart/technical reading and news/fundamental reading, producing their own buy score alongside the breakout score) is a materially bigger step, discussed with the user but NOT started -- see the new item 16 below for the concrete design questions that need answering first.

16. Multi-agent independent scoring (NOT DONE, design-only). User asked directly whether multiple agents (one reading charts, one reading news, etc.) could develop their OWN buy score, independent of the breakout Trade_Score. Reasoned response, not yet built:

    The technical build is straightforward -- a "chart agent" (rendered price/volume chart image fed to a vision-capable model) and a "news agent" (headlines/fundamentals fed to a text model), each scoring a ticker independently, combined into an AI_Score field displayed alongside Trade_Score (NOT blended into it).

    The hard problem is validation, and it's harder here than it was for RSI or breakout. Every signal this project has ever trusted (RSI, breakout, all its filters) went through the SAME gate before being taken seriously: a random-entry benchmark and/or ticker-holdout backtest proving the signal beats picking days/tickers at random. An AI_Score needs the identical bar before it should influence a real decision -- "it sounds smart" is exactly the trap the RSI investigation exposed (a plausible, confidently-tuned signal that carried zero real information). Backtesting an AI_Score honestly requires:
      a. Point-in-time chart images -- renderable from existing OHLCV history for any historical date, no real obstacle.
      b. Point-in-time NEWS text -- the real blocker. yfinance's .news only returns CURRENT headlines, not an archive of what was published as of a past date. Without genuine point-in-time news, "backtesting" the news agent on history would let it see (or the underlying model's training data would already contain) information from AFTER the simulated date -- exactly the look-ahead bias item 9 flagged, now concretely blocking the news half of this specific idea.
      c. Determinism/cost/speed at backtest scale -- fine for a live scan (a few signals a day), a real obstacle for re-running years of history across many trials the way Optuna does today.

    Recommended path if this gets picked up: run AI_Score LIVE-ONLY alongside Trade_Score for real time going forward (log both to MongoDB on every scan, not just Strong Buy/Buy), accumulating genuine forward-looking data -- effectively "paper trading" the AI score. After enough elapsed time/samples, apply the SAME random-entry-style validation this project has used for every other signal (does AI_Score's ranking predict real subsequent outcomes better than a random ranking?) before ever considering it as an input to real Trade_Score or capital allocation. This is slower than building a scored blend today, but it's the only way to know whether the AI score carries real information rather than just sounding informed -- the central, hard-won lesson of this entire session. Not started; needs the user's go-ahead on this design (and ideally a resolved point-in-time news data source) before implementation begins. DEPRIORITIZED for now -- user asked to put the agentic avenue on the backburner in favor of more mechanical accuracy work (see item 17).

17. [DONE, does NOT beat v19] ADX (trend-strength) filter on the breakout signal -- the first genuinely new complementary indicator explored after the RSI-overbought/relative-strength/volume-confirmation trio already in v19. User asked for other complementary algorithms; ADX was picked as the most orthogonal option (it measures how STRONG the current trend is, independent of direction -- a different dimension than RSI's momentum level, Relative_Strength's direction-vs-market, or Volume_Ratio's single-day volume spike).

    Built following the identical pattern as every other breakout filter: new config.adx_window (14 default) / config.breakout_adx_min (0.0/disabled default) fields, ADX computed via pandas_ta in precompute_breakout_frame() (vectorized, same "~260-day buffer is enough for Wilder-smoothing convergence" reasoning already verified for RSI/ATR), gated identically in simulate_breakout_signals (backtest) and add_breakout_trade_score (live) so the two can't silently disagree, added as a 7th tunable dimension to optimize.py's breakout search space (0.0-40.0 -- 25+ is "trending" and 40+ is "strong trend" in classical TA terms, so this range spans disabled through a genuinely demanding bar). Verified: unit tests on synthetic data (a strong-uptrend series produced ADX=74.5 vs. a choppy/sideways series' ADX=16.1 -- confirms the indicator correctly separates the two regimes as intended; a high breakout_adx_min correctly zeroed out a choppy-trend signal's Trade_Score while the disabled default left it untouched; missing/NaN ADX correctly does NOT exclude a signal on its own). AppTest clean on both the real active v19 config (exercises the new ADX column with the filter at its disabled default) and a monkeypatched config with breakout_adx_min=20.0 actually engaged.

    Full-scale result (152 tickers, 112 tune / 40 holdout, 30 trials, same window/methodology as v19/v20): best trial -- breakout_lookback_days=78, atr_take_profit_multiplier=2.15, stop_loss_atr_multiplier=1.74, breakout_rsi_overbought_threshold=74.15, breakout_relative_strength_min=-41.99, breakout_volume_ratio_min=2.08, breakout_adx_min=4.44 (barely above disabled -- the search found almost no ADX filtering helpful). Written as candidate v22. On its own holdout: win_rate=46.27%, sharpe_like=0.242, effective_trade_count=27.7 -- a notably THIN sample, barely above the ~15-trade trust floor and far smaller than v19's 108.7-161.1 across its various holdout evaluations.

    Re-scored v19's exact stored params against this SAME new 40-ticker holdout split (same fair-comparison discipline as the v20 check): win_rate=70.81%, sharpe_like=0.248, effective_trade_count=161.1. v19 wins clearly on win_rate and sample size; sharpe_like is nearly tied (0.248 vs 0.242) but on a far less trustworthy sample for v22. v22 NOT promoted -- doesn't beat v19.

    Interpretation: this is now the SECOND consecutive fresh 30-trial search on the expanded watchlist (after v20) that failed to beat v19 -- a real, if modest, data point that v19 is a genuinely robust config, not a lucky one. Whether ADX specifically carries no value, or whether 30 trials simply isn't enough to explore a 7-dimensional space thoroughly (the same under-exploration caveat raised for v16 and v20), remains open -- the near-disabled breakout_adx_min=4.44 the search landed on is consistent with either explanation. Not recommending further trial-budget escalation on this exact question without a specific reason to believe ADX in particular is being shortchanged; other complementary indicators (Bollinger Band squeeze, OBV volume-trend) or the multi-objective/drawdown-aware tuning approach may be a better use of the next round of search compute. v19 remains active; nothing promoted.

18. [DONE, inconclusive -- nominal win on a sample too thin to trust] On-Balance Volume (OBV) accumulation z-score and Bollinger Band volatility-squeeze z-score, the two complementary indicators the user asked for by name after ADX came up empty. Both target dimensions ADX/RSI/relative-strength/volume-ratio don't cover: OBV_Zscore reads sustained buying pressure BUILDING UP over weeks (not a single day's volume spike like Volume_Ratio), Squeeze_Zscore reads whether volatility was unusually CONTRACTED right before the breakout (the classic "coiled spring" pattern) -- a volatility-regime signal distinct from momentum, direction, trend-strength, or volume.

    Design notes worth recording: OBV is a raw CUMULATIVE sum (unlike RSI/ATR/ADX's Wilder-smoothing or SMA's fixed window) with no decay at all, so its absolute magnitude is arbitrary -- it depends on how much leading history precedes the window. Rather than use it raw, both OBV and the Bollinger volatility measure are z-scored against their own trailing rolling mean/stdev (config.obv_window=20, config.bb_squeeze_window=60) before gating -- proved (not just argued) invariant to leading-history length, since OBV and its rolling mean shift by the identical constant offset for any amount of extra history, canceling in the difference. Squeeze_Zscore is additionally read as of the PRIOR day (`.shift(1)`) so today's own breakout move can't inflate the volatility reading and mask the squeeze that preceded it -- same no-look-ahead-into-today convention Highest_High already used. New config.breakout_obv_zscore_min (-100.0/disabled default) and config.breakout_squeeze_zscore_max (100.0/disabled default), gated identically in simulate_breakout_signals/add_breakout_trade_score, added as 8th/9th dimensions to optimize.py's search space (both -3.0 to 3.0, spanning realistic z-score extremes). Verified: unit tests on synthetic data (a recent high-volume accumulation burst after a decline produced OBV_Zscore=1.61; a recent volatility contraction after a volatile stretch produced Squeeze_Zscore=-2.53 -- both indicators correctly read the intended regime; both gates correctly zero out a signal that can't clear a demanding bar while leaving disabled defaults untouched; missing/NaN values on either field correctly don't exclude a signal on their own). AppTest clean on both the real active v19 config and a monkeypatched config with both filters actually engaged.

    Full-scale result (152 tickers, 112 tune / 40 holdout, 45 trials -- raised from 30 given a 9D space and the lesson from item 17 that 30 trials struggled to explore 7D well): best trial -- breakout_lookback_days=45 (matches v19 exactly), atr_take_profit_multiplier=2.44, stop_loss_atr_multiplier=4.98, breakout_rsi_overbought_threshold=74.97, breakout_relative_strength_min=-14.95, breakout_volume_ratio_min=0.02 (~disabled), breakout_adx_min=18.71, breakout_obv_zscore_min=1.46, breakout_squeeze_zscore_max=-1.46. Unlike ADX, BOTH new filters landed at genuinely demanding, non-disabled values this time -- the search clearly found them worth engaging. Written as candidate v24. On its own holdout: win_rate=61.84%, sharpe_like=0.481, effective_trade_count=16.1 -- a nominally strong sharpe_like, but the sample is barely above MIN_TRADES_FOR_SCORE's 15-trade floor (the search itself printed the thin-sample warning), an order of magnitude smaller than v19's ~161-trade holdout sample. That thinness is a direct, expected consequence of the search finding two simultaneously-restrictive filters worth engaging together -- fewer, more selective signals by construction, not a bug.

    Re-scored v19's exact stored params against this SAME new 40-ticker holdout split (same fair-comparison discipline as items 14/17): win_rate=70.81%, sharpe_like=0.248, effective_trade_count=161.1. Direct comparison: v24's point-estimate sharpe_like (0.481) is nominally almost double v19's (0.248), but on a sample ~10x smaller and thinner than the trust floor was designed to guard against -- a sharpe ratio computed from 16 effective trades carries enough sampling noise that this difference is NOT distinguishable from noise with any confidence. This project has hit this exact "small-sample flip" trap before (the catalyst-warning finding and the survivorship-bias check both initially showed a result that reversed at a larger sample) -- the honest read here is INCONCLUSIVE, not a win. v24 NOT promoted; v19 remains active. If this combination is worth re-testing, the right next step is a LARGER holdout fraction or more tickers specifically for the OBV+squeeze combination (to get enough trades to trust the sharpe_like reading), not treating the current nominal win at face value.

19. [DONE] Risk-based position sizing. Was: Shares_To_Buy was always a flat dollar amount (Position Budget) divided by price -- the same $250 bet on a calm blue-chip and a violently volatile name, meaning the actual $ amount at risk if stopped out varied wildly and invisibly across trades depending on each ticker's own ATR/stop distance. This became a live, practical concern once v19 (stop_loss_atr_multiplier=3.73) replaced v3 (0.5) as the active config -- a much wider stop means a much bigger $ swing per trade under flat sizing at the same position budget, a caveat explicitly flagged (but not fixed) when v19 was promoted.

    Fixed: new swingtrade.size_by_risk(buy_price, stop_loss, risk_amount, fractional_share_decimals) (swingtrade/allocation.py) -- vectorized, pure, sizes shares so shares x (buy_price - stop_loss) is approximately risk_amount, the dollar amount you're willing to lose if the stop is hit, held roughly constant across every trade regardless of the ticker's own volatility. Falls back to 0 shares (not inf/NaN) for the degenerate risk_per_share <= 0 case (ATR rounds to 0, flat price action) -- same guard RRR already applies to the identical situation. A personal risk-management choice, not a signal property, so (like max_sector_allocation_pct) it's never called from swingtrade/backtest.py or optimize.py -- doesn't touch signal generation or backtested Sharpe at all.

    Wired in as a toggle, not a replacement, preserving the existing flat-budget behavior as the default: dip_buy_analyzer.py's sidebar gained a "Position sizing mode" radio (Flat $ per trade / Risk-based) that swaps the "Position Budget ($)" input for "Risk per Trade ($)"; ingest.py gained an optional --risk-amount flag (falls back to the existing --position-budget flat behavior when omitted). Both call the same swingtrade.size_by_risk() so the two entry points can't drift.

    Verified: unit tests (normal case correct, degenerate risk_per_share=0 correctly gives 0 shares, dollar risk actually held constant across two trades with very different stop distances). Real end-to-end check against live data (NVDA/AAPL/MSFT/AMD/JPM): Dollar_Risk came out EXACTLY $25.00 for every single ticker despite stop distances ranging from $29 (NVDA) to $149 (AMD, ~5x wider) -- Est_Cost varied proportionally (98.06 to 333.43) instead of being flat, exactly the intended behavior. AppTest confirmed the sidebar toggle renders correctly and swaps input fields with no exceptions. Real ingest.py --risk-amount 25 run against the full 152-ticker watchlist completed cleanly, 0 exceptions.

20. [DONE] Research-tier (Watch) live signal tracking -- decouples the live feedback loop's data-volume growth from real trading capacity, which was the actual open question after the "biggest impact avenue" discussion. Was: settle_trades.py already grades EVERY logged signal against real subsequent price action whether or not it was actually traded (confirmed_filled was always a reporting split, not a data-collection gate -- 84 of the 86 live Trade_Outcomes to date were never real trades) -- so the live loop was NEVER bottlenecked by trading capacity in the first place. It WAS bottlenecked by what got logged at all: storage/signals.py's LOGGABLE_SIGNALS only ever included Strong Buy/Buy, and a selective config like v19 fires those rarely (0 on most days scanned this session), so the sample was growing very slowly regardless of how many trades got confirmed.

    Fixed: Trade_Signals documents gained a "tier" field -- "actionable" (Strong Buy/Buy, unchanged real behavior, capital-allocation-eligible) or "research" (Watch, newly logged and settled purely to accumulate outcome data faster, NEVER capital-allocation-eligible). storage.log_trade_signals() now logs both tiers (return type changed from an int to {"actionable": n, "research": m}, both call sites -- ingest.py, dip_buy_analyzer.py -- updated to report the split). storage.get_signals_pending_confirmation() stays scoped to actionable only (research-tier Watch signals were never meant to be traded/confirmed). Tier is carried from Trade_Signals through settle_trades.py into Trade_Outcomes (storage.log_trade_outcome gained a tier param), and both settle_trades.py's summary and optimize.py's report_live_outcomes_context() now report actionable/research pooled metrics separately (research pooled with actionable would understate real trade quality, since research is deliberately a lower, noisier bar) -- pre-existing Trade_Outcomes/Trade_Signals documents written before this field existed default to "actionable" via .get("tier", "actionable"), correctly reflecting that every signal logged before this WAS actionable.

    Capital allocation is provably unaffected: allocate_capital() only ever acts on rows where Signal is Strong Buy/Buy (a hard `if signals[i] not in (...): continue` skip for everything else, Watch included), a design already in place before this change -- confirmed by code review, not just assumed.

    Verified end-to-end against the real MongoDB instance (not just synthetic unit tests): a real Watch-tier row logged via storage.log_trade_signals() came back with tier="research" exactly as expected; a mixed actionable+research batch produced the correct {"actionable": 1, "research": 1} split; get_signals_pending_confirmation() correctly included the actionable test row and excluded the research one; settle_one() (using real AAPL price history) correctly carried tier="research" from the source Trade_Signals doc through to the resulting Trade_Outcomes doc; optimize.report_live_outcomes_context() against real production data correctly bucketed all 86 pre-existing outcomes as "actionable" (backward-compat default) and reported "Research tier: 0" (accurate -- no research signals have accumulated yet, this feature just shipped). All test documents cleaned up afterward. AppTest confirmed the dashboard's updated log_trade_signals call site (dict unpacking) has no exceptions.

21. [DONE, code side -- Task Scheduler registration is a separate, deliberate step] Automation. Was: miss a day of ingest.py/settle_trades.py and you have a silent gap in the training data, with nothing telling you it happened -- the practical daily-use pain point behind items 1 and 20 both. Fixed: new daily_run.py runs ingest.py then settle_trades.py back-to-back as SUBPROCESSES (isolated -- a crash or sys.exit() in either can't take daily_run.py itself down, only the real exit code matters), and posts a Discord webhook report EVERY run (not just failures -- see below) via new notifications.py, which reads DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL from .env and degrades to a printed warning (never raises) if unconfigured, same fallback philosophy as MongoDB/Gemini connectivity. Safe to run more than once a day or on non-trading days -- both underlying scripts are upsert-based/idempotent, so an extra run just harmlessly confirms nothing new happened.

    Follow-up same day: user asked what the automated run actually returns, and specifically whether the full printed output (not just a failure summary) could show up in Discord -- worth doing given local Task Scheduler captures NO output anywhere by default (unlike GitHub Actions, which keeps full logs in its own UI), so Discord was the only realistic way to get that visibility for the Task-Scheduler path. Added notifications.notify_with_file() -- posts a short status line as the message body (still capped at Discord's 2000-char limit, it's meant to be a one-line status) plus the FULL combined stdout/stderr of both steps as an attached .txt file (multipart/form-data upload, no practical size limit for a normal run's output, well under Discord's ~8MB webhook attachment cap) -- avoids both truncating real output and the ugliness of splitting one report across many 2000-char messages. daily_run.py now calls this on every run regardless of outcome (OK or FAILED status line), not just on failure -- turns the Discord channel into the primary observability layer for whichever schedule path (GitHub Actions or local Task Scheduler) is actually used.

    Verified: notifications.notify()/notify_with_file() both confirmed to degrade gracefully (print, return False, never raise) with no webhook configured. daily_run.run_step() confirmed to capture a deliberately-failing subprocess's FULL (untruncated) combined output correctly. daily_run.main()'s success AND failure paths both tested directly (mocked run_step) -- correctly build the status message/filename/full-content attachment and call notify_with_file() with the right arguments either way, correct exit codes (0/1). The multipart request SHAPE itself verified against Discord's real API (a syntactically-valid but nonexistent webhook URL) -- got a clean 404 (webhook not found) rather than a 400 (malformed request), confirming the multipart/form-data construction itself is valid Discord API usage, not just locally self-consistent. Real, non-mocked end-to-end run of the underlying ingest.py+settle_trades.py subprocess flow against the full 152-ticker watchlist + all unsettled live signals: both steps completed successfully (exit 0), ~275s total (run before the notify_with_file changes; the subprocess-calling logic itself is unchanged, only the reporting layer changed, so not re-run at full scale afterward -- the mocked tests above cover the changed logic directly).

    Explicit known limitation, not silently assumed away: this can only report a failure from WITHIN a run that actually started. If the OS-level scheduler itself never fires (task disabled, PC off/asleep at the scheduled time), nothing here can detect that -- would need Task Scheduler's own History tab checked manually, or a separate external dead-man's-switch service (e.g. healthchecks.io's free tier) to close that specific gap. Not built; flagged as a real gap rather than papered over.

    Registering the actual schedule (the "does this run automatically" half) is a deliberate, separate step from writing the code -- a persistent, real change, not something to do silently. User asked specifically whether this could run even if their PC is off -- answered honestly: no local Task Scheduler mechanism can run anything on a fully powered-off machine (a hardware reality, not a Task Scheduler limitation); Wake-on-sleep only covers the sleep/hibernate case, not a true shutdown. Given the repo already lives on GitHub, added .github/workflows/daily_run.yml -- a GitHub Actions scheduled workflow (weekdays, 21:35 UTC, safely after US market close in either DST state) that runs daily_run.py on GitHub's own infrastructure, completely independent of the local machine's power state. Free (public repos unlimited; private repos ~2000 free min/month against a ~5 min/day job). Needs MONGODB_URI/DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL added as GitHub repository secrets (Settings -> Secrets and variables -> Actions) before it can actually connect to anything -- YAML syntax verified (parses correctly; PyYAML's default loader reinterprets the bare `on:` key as a boolean, a well-known, harmless artifact of generic YAML 1.1 parsing that does NOT affect GitHub's own workflow parser, which treats `on:` as a schema keyword regardless -- confirmed this is the standard, universal way every real GitHub Actions workflow is written, not a bug introduced here). Local Task Scheduler registration remains documented as an alternative/supplement for anyone who'd rather keep secrets off GitHub entirely and is fine with "doesn't run if the PC is off." See TUTORIAL.md section 9 for both paths' exact setup steps.

    Registered and live-tested both paths same day: local Task Scheduler (TradingSandbox-DailyRun, weekdays 5:00 PM, StartWhenAvailable so a missed run fires as soon as the user is next logged in rather than waiting for the next scheduled day -- verified directly against the task's own registered Settings/Principal, not assumed: StartWhenAvailable=True, LogonType=Interactive, DisallowStartIfOnBatteries=True/StopIfGoingOnBatteries=True -- the last two only matter on a laptop running unplugged, confirmed not a concern for this user's desktop) and GitHub Actions (pushed + repo secrets configured by the user). First real trigger of each surfaced a genuine bug -- see item 22.

22. [DONE] Real bug found via live-testing the automation from item 21: yfinance/Yahoo occasionally publishes the most recent trading day's bar with a real Volume but a NaN Close -- the day's data isn't fully finalized yet at fetch time. A NaN anywhere in a rolling window NaNs out every indicator computed through that row (SMA/RSI/ATR), which surfaced as `is_market_uptrend()` raising "insufficient history to compute 200-day SMA" despite `yf.download('SPY', period='1y', ...)` genuinely returning 251 real rows -- confirmed directly (`df['Close'].iloc[-1]` was NaN while `df['Volume'].iloc[-1]` was a real number). This broke the very first step of every live scan (the macro-uptrend gate, checked before any watchlist ticker), so it looked identical to a total live-path failure.

    Root-cause path, and an honest correction: the FIRST real trigger of the GitHub Actions workflow (item 21) hit this exact error, and it was initially (wrongly) diagnosed as Yahoo Finance blocking/rate-limiting cloud-datacenter IP ranges -- a real, well-documented, and superficially very plausible phenomenon (confirmed via web search against yfinance's own GitHub issues, which describe an ongoing pattern since Nov 2024). That diagnosis looked reasonable at the time but turned out to be WRONG: the identical error then reproduced on a direct LOCAL interactive run too (the user's own residential IP, the same environment that had worked correctly all session) -- which a genuine IP-blocking explanation cannot account for. Restating this plainly rather than quietly fixing it and moving on: the earlier IP-blocking diagnosis was incorrect, and the real cause was this NaN-Close data-quality issue, present equally in both environments.

    Fixed in market_data.fetch_data() (the one shared fetch function used by both the live dashboard and ingest.py, for SPY and every watchlist ticker alike): after fetching, drop any trailing rows where Close is NaN, looping in case more than one trailing row is affected, before returning. Raises a clear RuntimeError only if EVERY row turns out to be unusable (vs. the old behavior of silently computing on placeholder data and failing confusingly downstream).

    Verified three independent ways: (1) direct swingtrade/market_data.check_market_uptrend() call, now returns a clean (True, 747.03, 700.39) instead of raising; (2) a real, non-mocked `py -3 ingest.py` run against the full 152-ticker watchlist completed cleanly (109/152 analyzed, correct per-ticker macro-downtrend skips using real Last_Close/SMA200 values, no macro-trend error); (3) a real Task Scheduler trigger of the now-registered TradingSandbox-DailyRun task (Start-ScheduledTask, not a direct script invocation) completed with LastTaskResult=0 (success) -- the actual end-to-end path this whole item exists to validate. GitHub Actions was very likely hit by this same bug (not IP blocking) given the identical error message and the fact it's the same shared fetch function -- worth re-running to confirm now that the fix is in, expected to also resolve now that this ships.

23. [DONE, infrastructure sound -- this run's candidate does NOT beat v19] Multi-objective/drawdown-aware tuning. Was: optimize.py always optimized a single scalar (sharpe_like), completely blind to tail risk -- a config could "win" by juicing average consistency while quietly tolerating a nastier worst-case drawdown, and nothing in the search would ever surface that. Given three consecutive single-axis filter searches (ADX, OBV, squeeze -- items 17/18) all came up short or inconclusive against v19, this was picked as the next avenue specifically because it's a genuinely different LEVER (methodology, not another indicator) rather than another attempt at the same axis.

    Built: swingtrade.compute_max_drawdown(trades) -- max peak-to-trough decline (%) of a SEQUENTIAL equity curve built by compounding resolved trades in chronological (entry_date) order. Explicitly a simplification, stated plainly in its own docstring: this engine doesn't track concurrent-position capital allocation, so trades that genuinely overlapped in real calendar time are treated as if taken one after another -- a standard, defensible proxy for loss-clustering, not a literal "what would my account balance have done" simulation. swingtrade.flatten_out_sample_trades() pools a fold-result list into the plain chronological trade list this needs (separate from summarize_weighted()'s recency-weighted pooling, which needs per-fold weight association compute_max_drawdown doesn't use). optimize.py gained --multi-objective (off by default): when set, the Optuna objective returns (sharpe_like, max_drawdown) instead of a scalar, `optuna.create_study(directions=["maximize","minimize"])` searches for the Pareto front instead of a single winner, and main() selects the highest-sharpe_like trial among the Pareto-optimal set that still clears MIN_TRADES_FOR_SCORE to actually write as a candidate (the full front is printed either way, so the tradeoffs that got traded away are visible, not hidden).

    Verified rigorously given this touches the shared objective-building code every search depends on: unit tests on compute_max_drawdown() (hand-computed drawdown on a synthetic pnl sequence matched exactly; monotonic gains correctly produce 0% drawdown; empty/all-OPEN trades correctly return None; result independent of input list order, confirming the internal chronological sort works). Byte-for-byte backward-compatibility proof for the DEFAULT (non-multi-objective) path: stashed the changes, ran a real search, unstashed, re-ran the identical command -- diffed the two outputs (ignoring only timestamps/study UUIDs, both expected non-deterministic) and got an EXACT match, confirming --multi-objective being absent changes nothing about existing behavior. Multi-objective mode itself smoke-tested at small scale (correct tuple values, correct Pareto-front reporting, correct graceful "no eligible trial" handling with no crash).

    Full-scale result (152 tickers, 112 tune / 40 holdout, 45 trials, same window/methodology as v19 through v25): the Pareto front collapsed to a SINGLE dominant trial (breakout_lookback_days=16, atr_take_profit_multiplier=2.77, stop_loss_atr_multiplier=3.12, breakout_rsi_overbought_threshold=92.57, breakout_relative_strength_min=-28.78, breakout_volume_ratio_min=0.97, breakout_adx_min=35.39 [notably demanding -- recall item 17 found ADX alone got pushed toward disabled; here, combined with other filters, the search found a highly restrictive ADX bar useful], breakout_obv_zscore_min=0.67, breakout_squeeze_zscore_max=-1.39) -- sharpe_like=0.552, max_drawdown=13.14% on TUNE, which LOOKED like a clean win on both axes simultaneously. Written as candidate v25.

    But the TUNE sample was already thin (effective_trade_count=18.7, barely above the 15-trade floor), and holdout was worse: effective_trade_count=5.5 (well below the floor, the search's own WARN caught this), win_rate=19.16%, sharpe_like=-0.694 (NEGATIVE). Re-scored v19 on the identical new 40-ticker holdout split for a fair fight (same discipline as every prior candidate): v19 win_rate=70.81%, sharpe_like=0.248, effective_trade_count=161.1, max_drawdown=61.39% -- v25 win_rate=19.16%, sharpe_like=-0.694, effective_trade_count=5.5, max_drawdown=20.53%. v19 wins decisively on the metric that actually matters (sharpe_like, win_rate, sample size); v25's attractive-looking LOWER drawdown (20.53% vs 61.39%) is a mechanical artifact of having only 6 total trades on holdout instead of 443 -- far too few observations to ever accumulate a real drawdown, not evidence of genuine robustness. v25 NOT promoted; v19 remains active.

    Real, useful methodological lesson worth recording plainly, not just the negative result: max_drawdown as built here is NOT floor-gated by effective_trade_count the way sharpe_like already is via the under-sampled penalty -- so a multi-objective search can gravitate toward extreme selectivity purely because fewer trades mechanically look "safer" on the drawdown axis, the same failure mode a naive single-objective search has toward a lucky small sample, just wearing a different metric's clothes. The infrastructure (compute_max_drawdown, Pareto selection, --multi-objective) is sound and thoroughly tested -- this particular run's result just wasn't trustworthy. Worth revisiting with the drawdown objective ALSO subject to the same MIN_TRADES_FOR_SCORE-style gate sharpe_like already has (e.g. penalize -- return a bad drawdown value -- for under-sampled trials on the drawdown axis too, which the current code already does via UNDER_SAMPLED_DRAWDOWN_PENALTY for the fully-under-sampled case, but doesn't yet do for a trial that clears the floor on TUNE while still being thin), or with a larger trial budget to give the search more chances to find a genuinely low-drawdown config that ALSO holds up on a healthy sample size, not a future session priority by default but flagged here for whoever picks this back up.

[DONE] Signal-drought fixes -- user flagged too many consecutive days with zero signals across every tier (Strong Buy through Watch), correctly identifying this as a real practical usability problem worth addressing regardless of whether v19's underlying selectivity is statistically justified. Root cause, explained honestly before building anything: add_breakout_trade_score()'s hard gate (`Trade_Score = raw_score.where(eligible, 0.0)`) requires Breakout_Signal=True (a genuine new N-day closing high, TODAY) before ANY score -- even Watch -- can be nonzero. Combined with the macro-downtrend gate excluding a meaningful chunk of the watchlist on any given day (repeatedly observed at 30-43 of 152 tickers this session), the pool of tickers even ELIGIBLE to break out shrinks further. This is the direct, structural cost of v19's validated selectivity, not a bug -- but a real dry spell is still a real dry spell.     a. Zero risk to the validated strategy: doesn't change a single threshold, purely gives v19 more surface area to find a real signal.

    b. New swingtrade.loosened_breakout_config() (swingtrade/config.py) -- takes any breakout TradingConfig and returns a copy with the six "sharpening" filters (breakout_rsi_overbought_threshold, breakout_relative_strength_min, breakout_volume_ratio_min, breakout_adx_min, breakout_obv_zscore_min, breakout_squeeze_zscore_max) reset to their practical-disabled defaults, while keeping the core trigger definition (breakout_lookback_days, ATR stop/target multiples) identical. dip_buy_analyzer.py gained a new, ALWAYS-visible (for breakout configs, not gated behind a checkbox) "Loosened Filters View" section directly below the normal Scan Results table -- re-scores the SAME already-fetched scan data (no extra fetch/API cost) under the loosened config, showing what would score a signal under just the base strategy. Explicitly, repeatedly labeled informational-only: never touches capital allocation, never gets logged to MongoDB, the real Scan Results table above it remains the only thing driving real decisions. Verified: a synthetic row engineered to fail every one of v19's six extra filters correctly scores 0/Ignore under the real config and a real nonzero Buy-tier score under the loosened one, proving the re-scoring logic actually works rather than trivially passing everything through. AppTest against the full new 253-ticker watchlist (real active v19 config) confirmed no exceptions and the new section renders with the correct subheader. Real ingest.py run against the expanded watchlist confirmed the live headless path also works cleanly at the new scale (this feature is dashboard-only, ingest.py doesn't need a "second window" concept the same way).

    a. That part was working as intended and needed no change.
    b. What WAS genuinely broken: real-world validation data (comparing live outcomes to what the backtest predicts) had completely stopped accumulating, because even the item-24 "research" tier (Watch) requires the same hard Breakout_Signal=True gate as actionable tiers -- v19's selectivity was starving its own research-tier safety net, not just its actionable signals.

    Fix: a third tier, tier="research_loosened", reusing item 24's swingtrade.loosened_breakout_config() -- but now logged to MongoDB instead of display-only, specifically for rows the ACTIVE config scored Ignore that the LOOSENED config scores Strong Buy/Buy/Watch.     Implementation: storage/signals.py's log_trade_signal()/log_trade_signals() gained an optional `tier` param that FORCES the tier regardless of the row's own Signal value (a loosened-config row can carry signal="Buy" while still being tier="research_loosened", never actionable) -- avoids a subtle bug where a naive implementation keyed tier off Signal alone and a loosened "Strong Buy" would incorrectly look real. get_signals_pending_confirmation() was tightened to filter on tier explicitly (ACTIONABLE_TIER_FILTER, matching missing-tier legacy docs too) instead of just Signal value, specifically so a research_loosened row can never show up as something to confirm/trade. [DONE, NOT VALIDATED -- new "pullback" strategy built and tested, does NOT clear the bar, not wired into any live path] User wanted more consistent trading opportunities/capital deployed, given how rarely v19's fresh-N-day-high trigger fires (item 24/25 already showed loosening the extra filters doesn't help on days literally nothing makes a new high anywhere). Requested direction (chosen via AskUserQuestion over MA-crossover and volume-spike alternatives): "pullback-in-uptrend" -- buy a shallow dip toward a rising short-term SMA within a confirmed macro uptrend, a fundamentally less restrictive trigger than a fresh extreme, so it should fire far more often.

    Built as a full third strategy (strategy="pullback"), mirroring every existing convention: swingtrade/config.py gained 3 new fields (pullback_ma_window=20, pullback_ma_slope_window=10, pullback_band_pct=3.0 defaults); swingtrade/levels.py gained precompute_pullback_frame()/pullback_levels_from_frame()/compute_pullback_levels() (schema-compatible output, same NaN/macro-downtrend/liquidity gates as the other two); swingtrade/scoring.py gained add_pullback_trade_score() (same rrr_score+distance_score hard-gate pattern as add_breakout_trade_score()); swingtrade/backtest.py gained simulate_pullback_signals()/simulate_random_pullback_entries() (reuses the RSI strategy's _find_entry_fill() resting-limit-order mechanics, not breakout's stop-buy) plus a 3-way strategy dispatch in run_backtest(); optimize.py and benchmark_random_entry.py both gained a "pullback" --strategy choice with matching search space/dispatch.     Verified before any backtest was trusted: hand-computable unit tests (direct frame-row construction, mirroring the same synthetic-row technique used for breakout's hard-gate verification) confirmed Pullback_Signal/Trade_Score/Signal match hand-derived values exactly in a clear-signal case, a falling-MA rejection case, a too-far-from-MA rejection case, and a macro-downtrend RuntimeError case; a rolling-window unit test confirmed MA_Pullback/MA_Pullback_Prior's precompute matches manual rolling-mean arithmetic exactly. Live smoke test (5 real tickers) produced sane, explicable values with zero exceptions.

    Random-entry-timing benchmark (benchmark_random_entry.py --strategy pullback, the single most important check per this session's own established rigor -- see the RSI-oversold precedent), full 253-ticker watchlist, 2021-06-01..2026-07-26, ticker-holdout (190 tune/63 holdout, same split as every other search this session), UNTUNED defaults: HOLDOUT sharpe_like REAL=-0.019 vs RANDOM=+0.014 (win_rate 58.98% vs 59.44%) -- REAL loses, though mildly (near-zero either way, not RSI's dramatic loss). Flagged to the user as ambiguous rather than a clean pass/fail given how mild it was; user chose to still run the full search rather than stop on this alone.

    Full walk-forward + ticker-holdout Optuna search (optimize.py --strategy pullback, 30 trials, same window/methodology/holdout split as every v19-era search), a compact 5D space (pullback_ma_window, pullback_ma_slope_window, pullback_band_pct, atr_take_profit_multiplier, stop_loss_atr_multiplier -- deliberately smaller than breakout's 6-9D given this session's lesson that high-dimensional spaces under-explore on a 15-45 trial budget). Best trial (#20, sharpe_like=0.2 on TUNE): pullback_ma_window=22, pullback_ma_slope_window=13, pullback_band_pct=8.69 (near its 10.0 ceiling -- a very loose, barely-restrictive band), atr_take_profit_multiplier=2.27, stop_loss_atr_multiplier=4.95 (right at its 5.0 ceiling). Against DEFAULT_CONFIG's untuned baseline on the SAME 63 never-seen holdout tickers, this candidate looked like a real win: sharpe_like 0.098 vs baseline's -0.003, on an enormous effective_trade_count=2339.5 -- superficially the same "tune-set gain holds up on holdout" pattern that made v12/v14/v19 trustworthy.

    But beating an untuned version of ITSELF is a different claim than beating RANDOM entry timing, and both search parameters pinning at their range ceilings (band_pct, stop_loss_atr_multiplier) is exactly the boundary-pinning pattern this session has repeatedly read as "the search wants to go further" -- here, further would mean an almost-fully-disabled gate, i.e. Optuna finding value mostly by loosening pullback until it's closer to "any day in a confirmed uptrend" plus a wide stop that dampens whipsaws, not genuine dip-timing skill. Confirmed twice now (untuned and tuned), same conclusion both times: pullback-in-uptrend's ENTRY TIMING does not carry real predictive value beyond the payoff structure + macro-uptrend drift that a random day in the same gated universe already captures -- the same fate as RSI-oversold, not breakout's.

    Conclusion: infrastructure is sound and fully reusable (a third strategy slots into every existing tool -- WFO, Optuna, ticker-holdout, random-entry benchmark -- with zero changes to any of them), but THIS v1 signal design doesn't clear the bar. Written as candidate v26 in System_Config (status=candidate, NOT active) purely as a record of what was tested -- NOT promoted, and nothing wired into dip_buy_analyzer.py/ingest.py/market_data.py's live paths. v19 untouched throughout. A future v2 (e.g. requiring the pullback to follow a genuine prior extension rather than a wide symmetric band, or adding a real confirmation-of-turn condition instead of a static zone) is a plausible next avenue if revisited, but that's a new design decision for the user to make, not assumed here.

27. [DONE, VALIDATED -- new "breakout_retest" strategy built and tested, CLEARS the bar, candidate v27 written, NOT yet wired into any live path] Direct follow-up to item 26's negative result. Both prior signal families tested against random-entry timing this session had a common thread by result: RSI-oversold (weakness-anchored) and pullback-in-uptrend (MA-proximity-anchored) both LOST to matched-count random entry on holdout. Only breakout (v19, extreme-anchored -- a genuine fresh N-day closing high) beat it. Discussed several follow-up strategy directions with the user (52-week-high momentum, cross-sectional relative-strength ranking, volatility-squeeze breakout, post-earnings continuation); chose to pursue "breakout retest" first specifically because it keeps the one validated ingredient (a genuine recent breakout) while relaxing its single most restrictive property -- must fire the SAME DAY -- instead of introducing an unrelated signal family.

    Design: strategy="breakout_retest". Reuses precompute_breakout_frame() wholesale (not reimplemented) and adds 3 vectorized derived columns: Breakout_Flag (Close > Highest_High, same definition as v19's Breakout_Signal, computed for every row), Last_Breakout_Level (Highest_High forward-filled from the most recent Breakout_Flag day via .ffill() -- carries no look-ahead, same as every other rolling column in this codebase), and Days_Since_Breakout (row-position distance to that same day, same ffill-of-position technique). Retest_Signal requires 0 < Days_Since_Breakout <= retest_window_days (excludes the breakout's own day -- that's breakout's domain, not a retest) AND price within +/- retest_band_pct of that original level. Buy_Price is the ORIGINAL breakout's trigger level -- a resting LIMIT order via the same _find_entry_fill() mechanics as RSI/pullback (buying a dip back down to a known level), NOT breakout's own stop-buy _find_breakout_fill(). New TradingConfig fields: retest_window_days=10, retest_band_pct=3.0 (only 2 -- reuses breakout_lookback_days for what counts as "a breakout" and every existing ATR/stop field). Full parallel build across swingtrade/config.py, levels.py (precompute_breakout_retest_frame/breakout_retest_levels_from_frame/compute_breakout_retest_levels), scoring.py (add_breakout_retest_trade_score, same hard-gate pattern), backtest.py (simulate_breakout_retest_signals/simulate_random_breakout_retest_entries, 4-way run_backtest() dispatch), optimize.py and benchmark_random_entry.py (4th --strategy choice each).

    Verified before any backtest was trusted: hand-computable unit tests (direct frame-row construction, same technique as item 26) confirmed Retest_Signal/Trade_Score/Signal match hand-derived values exactly for a clear-retest case, a too-late case (past retest_window_days), a too-far case (outside retest_band_pct), a never-broken-out case (NaN fallback), and a macro-downtrend RuntimeError case. Separately, the vectorized Last_Breakout_Level/Days_Since_Breakout ffill() logic was checked against an independent brute-force reference implementation (plain Python loop over the already-trusted Highest_High/Close columns) across 60 synthetic rows with 2 engineered breakout events -- zero mismatches. Live smoke test (7 real tickers) produced sane, explicable values with zero exceptions, including correctly excluding a same-day breakout (Days_Since_Breakout=0) from Retest_Signal.

    Random-entry-timing benchmark (benchmark_random_entry.py --strategy breakout_retest), full 253-ticker watchlist, same 2021-06-01..2026-07-26/190-tune/63-holdout methodology as every prior search, UNTUNED defaults: HOLDOUT sharpe_like REAL=0.070 vs RANDOM=0.024 (win_rate 61.21% vs 60.45%), effective_trade_count=5955.7 -- REAL clearly and meaningfully ahead, the first time any strategy besides breakout itself has cleared this bar on untuned defaults.

    Full walk-forward + ticker-holdout Optuna search (optimize.py --strategy breakout_retest, 30 trials, same window/methodology/holdout split), a compact 5D space (breakout_lookback_days, retest_window_days, retest_band_pct, atr_take_profit_multiplier, stop_loss_atr_multiplier). Best trial (#21, sharpe_like=0.174 on TUNE): breakout_lookback_days=75, retest_window_days=21, retest_band_pct=8.84 (near its 10.0 ceiling), atr_take_profit_multiplier=1.46, stop_loss_atr_multiplier=4.98 (near its 5.0 ceiling) -- three parameters pinning near their range ceilings, the SAME boundary-pinning pattern that made item 26's pullback candidate a false positive, so this was NOT trusted on its own. Against baseline on the 63 never-seen holdout tickers: candidate sharpe_like=0.106 vs baseline's -0.017, win_rate 62.25% vs 41.29%, effective_trade_count=2601.2.

    Learning directly from item 26's mistake: re-ran the random-entry benchmark a SECOND time, loading candidate v27's actual tuned params directly from System_Config (storage.get_config_by_version(27), never touching v19's active status) instead of comparing only to DEFAULT_CONFIG. Result: HOLDOUT sharpe_like REAL=0.057 vs RANDOM=0.041 (win_rate 60.56% vs 60.70%, essentially matched -- the edge here is in sharpe_like/consistency, not raw win rate), on an enormous, well-matched sample (effective_trade_count 9542.5 REAL vs 9508.6 RANDOM). REAL beats RANDOM on ALL/TUNE/HOLDOUT cuts, at BOTH untuned defaults and the tuned candidate -- unlike pullback, this is not an artifact of loosening a gate until it approximates random-entry-in-uptrend; retest timing itself carries a real (modest, but consistent and well-sampled) edge.

    Conclusion: this is the first strategy besides breakout itself to clear the full validation bar this session (random-entry timing beaten twice, tune-to-holdout generalization held, huge trustworthy samples throughout). Written as candidate v27 in System_Config (status=candidate, NOT active) -- NOT promoted, and nothing wired into dip_buy_analyzer.py/ingest.py/market_data.py's live paths yet, per the scope agreed with the user before starting (build + validate first, live-dashboard wiring is a separate, later, explicitly-gated task). v19 untouched throughout. 52-week-high momentum remains queued as the next strategy to explore after this one.

28. [DONE, VALIDATED (modest edge) -- new "week52_high" strategy built and tested, CLEARS the bar, candidate v28 written, NOT yet wired into any live path] Direct follow-up to item 27, per the user's planned ordering (breakout_retest first, then 52-week-high momentum, then wire both). A well-documented academic factor (George & Hwang 2004, "The 52-Week High and Momentum Investing") distinct from every prior attempt: unlike breakout (a discrete "new high TODAY" event) or breakout_retest (a bounded window after one specific event), this is a continuous STATE -- how close is price, right now, to its own trailing week52_lookback_days (252 by default) high -- so it can stay true for many consecutive days while a stock consolidates near its highs. Still anchored to genuine strength, not weakness or MA proximity.

    Design: strategy="week52_high". Week52_High = High.rolling(week52_lookback_days).max() -- deliberately NOT shifted (unlike breakout's Highest_High): breakout's shift exists because it detects a discrete EVENT (did today cross a level established BEFORE today); this is a continuous STATE (how close is today's own price to its own trailing high, inclusive of today) -- the standard academic definition, still fully backtest-safe since both formulations only ever use data through `as_of`. Buy_Price is today's own Close (a resting LIMIT order at essentially the current price -- "already near strength," not "wait for a dip level"), filled via the same _find_entry_fill() as pullback/breakout_retest. Distance_to_Buy_Pct is repurposed to mean "distance BELOW the 52-week high" (>=0 normally, can go slightly negative on a fresh high) -- same "clip at 0, smaller=better" scoring formula as every other strategy. New TradingConfig fields: week52_lookback_days=252, week52_nearness_pct=5.0 (only 2, leanest search space yet -- no third dimension needed). Full parallel build across swingtrade/config.py, levels.py (precompute_week52_frame/week52_levels_from_frame/compute_week52_levels), scoring.py (add_week52_trade_score), backtest.py (simulate_week52_signals/simulate_random_week52_entries, 5-way run_backtest() dispatch), optimize.py and benchmark_random_entry.py (5th --strategy choice each).

    One real infrastructure fix needed along the way: run_backtest.py's LOOKBACK_BUFFER_DAYS (used by both optimize.py and benchmark_random_entry.py's fetch_history()) was hardcoded to 320 calendar days, sized for SMA200's ~290-calendar-day warmup with a ~30-day margin. week52_lookback_days=252 trading days needs ~365 calendar days -- the old buffer would have silently starved early-window Week52_High values of NaN for a meaningful stretch after window_start. Bumped to 420 calendar days (safe margin over 252 trading days), a global constant change that's harmless for the other four strategies (they just get a bit more unused leading history).

    Verified before any backtest was trusted: hand-computable unit tests (direct frame-row construction) confirmed Week52_Signal/Trade_Score/Signal match hand-derived values exactly for a clear near-high case, a too-far-below case, a fresh-high-today edge case (Distance_to_Buy_Pct=0, still a valid signal), and a macro-downtrend RuntimeError case. Separately verified the rolling Week52_High computation (inclusive of today) against a manual max() over a hand-constructed oscillating series. Live smoke test (7 real tickers) produced sane, explicable values with zero exceptions -- and confirmed this strategy fires far more often than any prior one (matches the "continuous state, not discrete event" design intent).

    Random-entry-timing benchmark (benchmark_random_entry.py --strategy week52_high), full 253-ticker watchlist, same methodology as every prior search, UNTUNED defaults: HOLDOUT sharpe_like REAL=0.070 vs RANDOM=0.056 (win_rate 62.19% vs 61.23%), effective_trade_count=10350.3 -- REAL ahead, and this strategy's signal COUNT (64,567 real signals) was the highest of any strategy tested this session, by a wide margin.

    Full walk-forward + ticker-holdout Optuna search (optimize.py --strategy week52_high, 30 trials, same window/methodology, a compact 4D space: week52_lookback_days, week52_nearness_pct, atr_take_profit_multiplier, stop_loss_atr_multiplier). Best trial (#19, sharpe_like=0.164 on TUNE): week52_lookback_days=140, week52_nearness_pct=3.81, atr_take_profit_multiplier=1.86, stop_loss_atr_multiplier=4.57 -- notably, UNLIKE both item 26's pullback candidate and item 27's breakout_retest candidate, these parameters are NOT pinned near their range ceilings (week52_lookback_days is well within 100-252, week52_nearness_pct well within 0.5-15.0; only stop_loss_atr_multiplier at ~91% of its range is on the higher side, not fully pinned) -- a better sign than either prior search, though still re-verified directly rather than trusted on the WFO score alone, per the standing rule from items 26/27. Against baseline on the 63 never-seen holdout tickers: candidate sharpe_like=0.096 vs baseline's 0.014 (note: baseline itself was already POSITIVE here, unlike pullback's/retest's negative untuned baselines), effective_trade_count=2479.8.

    Re-ran the random-entry benchmark a second time, loading candidate v28's actual tuned params directly from System_Config (storage.get_config_by_version(28), never touching v19's active status). Result: HOLDOUT sharpe_like REAL=0.071 vs RANDOM=0.065 (win_rate 52.89% vs 52.09%), on a huge, well-matched sample (effective_trade_count 9643.1 REAL vs 9605.0 RANDOM). REAL beats RANDOM on ALL/TUNE/HOLDOUT cuts at both untuned defaults and the tuned candidate -- a genuine pass, consistent with breakout_retest's result. Worth naming plainly: the margin here is noticeably THINNER than breakout_retest's (holdout gap 0.006 vs retest's 0.016), and the tuned candidate's margin over random is actually smaller (in relative terms) than the untuned defaults' margin was -- suggesting most of this strategy's value comes from the base "near a 52-week high" concept itself, with the Optuna tuning adding less incremental edge than it did for breakout_retest. Still real, still consistent, still backed by a huge sample -- just a more modest edge.

    Conclusion: second strategy (after breakout_retest) to clear the full validation bar this session. Written as candidate v28 in System_Config (status=candidate, NOT active) -- NOT promoted, nothing wired into dip_buy_analyzer.py/ingest.py/market_data.py's live paths yet. v19 untouched throughout. Per the user's plan, both breakout_retest (v27) and week52_high (v28) now move to the follow-up task: wiring both into the live dashboard as genuinely tradeable secondary signals alongside v19, each with its own separate cash pool.

29. [DONE] Wired breakout_retest (v27) and week52_high (v28) into the live dashboard as genuinely tradeable secondary signals alongside v19 -- the follow-up task explicitly planned after items 27/28 validated. Scope deliberately limited to dip_buy_analyzer.py (interactive dashboard) -- ingest.py/GitHub Actions automation for the two new strategies stays untouched, per the agreed plan to watch live dashboard behavior for a while first.

    Exploration before writing any dashboard code surfaced two real, necessary prerequisites, not just UI wiring:

    a. storage/signals.py's unique index was (ticker, signal_date) only -- if the same ticker fired under v19 AND v27/v28 the same day, logging the second would silently overwrite the first's document. storage/outcomes.py had the identical issue on Trade_Outcomes. Flagged to the user explicitly (AskUserQuestion) before proceeding; confirmed: fix it properly. Both collections' unique indexes migrated to (ticker, signal_date, strategy); _build_document()/log_trade_outcome() gained a top-level "strategy" field; mark_settled()/confirm_fill()/unconfirm_fill() gained a required strategy parameter; settle_trades.py passes config.strategy through (correct for old AND new docs, since TradingConfig.from_dict() already defaults missing "strategy" to "rsi"); confirm_fill.py gained a --strategy CLI arg and now prints each pending signal's strategy. One-time live-data migration run directly against the real Atlas cluster: backfilled "strategy": "rsi" onto all 177 Trade_Signals + 171 Trade_Outcomes documents missing the field (matches what from_dict() already inferred for them), then dropped both collections' old ticker_1_signal_date_1 unique index and created the new 3-field one. Verified: 0 documents left missing strategy after backfill; a synthetic test (ZZTEST_MULTISTRAT, cleaned up after) confirmed the same ticker/date logs 3 separate documents under 3 different strategies without collision, and that re-logging the same strategy still upserts in place (no duplicate growth).

    b. market_data.scan_tickers() only ever dispatched "breakout" vs. everything-else (compute_levels/RSI) -- it never learned about pullback/breakout_retest/week52_high, a real latent bug independent of this task (if the active config were ever one of those three, it would've silently scored as RSI). Also, fetch and per-ticker compute were tightly coupled in one loop -- OHLCV was fetched, scored, and discarded, never reusable, meaning 3 simultaneous strategy scans would have meant 3x the network fetch cost (likely 10+ minutes for a single dashboard load against the full 253-ticker watchlist). Fixed by splitting scan_tickers() into fetch_ticker_bundle() (pure fetch, strategy-independent, also fixes an existing bug where SPY was fetched twice for breakout configs) and score_bundle_for_strategy() (pure compute, now a full 5-way dispatch covering every strategy). scan_tickers() itself becomes a thin wrapper of the two -- signature and behavior UNCHANGED for existing single-strategy callers (ingest.py, confirmed via direct regression test: old vs. new scan_tickers produce byte-identical results field-for-field on real tickers for both rsi and breakout configs, and a real ingest.py run against the full watchlist completed with 0 exceptions afterward).

    New config_loader.load_config_by_version(version) mirrors load_active_config()'s shape but returns (None, reason) on failure rather than a silent DEFAULT_CONFIG fallback -- deliberately different from load_active_config's fallback philosophy, since substituting DEFAULT_CONFIG (strategy="rsi") for a missing breakout_retest/week52_high candidate would display a nonsensical mismatched section rather than a clear "unavailable" message.

    dip_buy_analyzer.py: replaced the old per-config-cached scan_watchlist() with cached_fetch_bundle() (cached on tickers alone, not config -- correct, since fetching doesn't depend on strategy), shared across all three sections. Two new sidebar "Total Available Cash" inputs (one per secondary strategy, clearly labeled, never merged with the primary pool or each other -- the user's explicit choice from earlier in the session). New render_secondary_section() helper runs the full score -> size -> log -> allocate -> display pipeline for each secondary strategy against the SAME shared bundle -- a leaner treatment than the primary section (no per-sector-breakdown expander, no Loosened Filters View) since these are newly-added, alongside-only signals, not the main view. existing_holdings (real portfolio) is shared and NOT updated between the three allocation passes within one page load -- each strategy's allocation is evaluated against actual holdings, not against what another strategy hypothetically proposed today, documented explicitly in the module docstring. The primary section's own scoring dispatch (previously the same 2-way breakout/else gap as market_data.py) was fixed to the same 5-way _score_for_strategy() helper, closing that latent bug in the live dashboard path too. Primary section's existing UI (4-metric row, sector-breakdown expander, Loosened Filters View) left completely unchanged in appearance -- only its data source was swapped to the shared bundle (verified behaviorally identical via the scan_tickers regression test above).

    Verified end-to-end: AppTest against the real dashboard (full 253-ticker watchlist, real active v19 config, real v27/v28 candidates loaded by version) completed with 0 exceptions. All 4 expected subheaders rendered ("Scan Results", "Loosened Filters View", "Breakout Retest Signals (v27, secondary)", "52-Week High Signals (v28, secondary)"). All three sections correctly logged to MongoDB via the real storage layer ("Logged 0 actionable + 0 research signal(s)" each -- an honest result, matching real current market data: nothing scored above Ignore under any of the three strategies today). Confirmed 3 independent "Total Available Cash" sidebar inputs and 3 independent allocation-summary metric rows render correctly, one per strategy.

30. [DONE, UNVALIDATED BY DESIGN -- new "LLM Agent" dashboard tab built, deliberately NOT capital-eligible, will remain unvalidated until a real prospective observation window passes] User found github.com/TauricResearch/TradingAgents (a multi-agent LLM trading framework -- separate fundamentals/sentiment/news/technical analyst agents, a bull/bear debate, a risk-management team, a portfolio-manager approval step) and asked for ideas/inspiration, then specifically asked whether an LLM-derived method could sit alongside the backtested strategies on its own tab. Answered honestly before building anything: an LLM-driven signal CANNOT be validated the way every strategy this session was (beats matched-count random-entry timing on 5 years of held-out history, via benchmark_random_entry.py) -- re-prompting a model today with "historical" context risks it already knowing what happened next from training data, contaminating any backtest with lookahead bias baked into the test itself, not filtered out by it. ai_context.py already sidesteps this exact problem by staying a pure informational summary, never touching Trade_Score, never called from the backtester. This item extends that same discipline to a genuine Buy/Hold/Avoid judgment instead of just a summary, but the consequence holds: this can ONLY be validated PROSPECTIVELY (real time passing, real settled trades), never backtested, and is treated as a hard constraint on the design throughout, not a caveat added after the fact.

    Scope decision (confirmed via AskUserQuestion): a single synthesis LLM call per candidate ticker, NOT a multi-agent pipeline mirroring TradingAgents' full analyst/debate/risk-team architecture -- matches the "lean v1" discipline every mechanical strategy this session started with (pullback/breakout_retest/week52_high all began minimal), and keeps cost/rate-limit exposure and surface-area-to-get-wrong low before this even reaches a validation window that already takes weeks to months.

    New llm_agent.py (sibling to ai_context.py, identical fallback philosophy -- is_available() gates on GEMINI_API_KEY + google-genai installed, evaluate_ticker() returns None rather than raising on any failure). Uses the same free-tier gemini-2.5-flash model as ai_context.py (no new cost), but with structured JSON output (response_mime_type="application/json") rather than free text, since this result needs to be machine-parsed into a Trade_Score, not just displayed -- confirmed the installed google-genai version supports response_mime_type/response_schema via snake_case kwargs before committing to this approach. _parse_response() validates the decision is one of exactly Buy/Hold/Avoid, confidence is numeric and in [0,100], and rationale is a non-empty string -- any other shape returns None, since a confidently-wrong JSON blob is not more trustworthy than no response at all.

    Inputs given to the model (bundled per candidate ticker): whatever's already computed this page load (Last_Close, RSI, ATR, each mechanical strategy's own Trade_Score for that ticker, Buy_Price/Stop_Loss/Sell_Price, Catalyst_Warning/next earnings date), recent headlines (market_data.get_multi_headlines, already used by ai_context.py), and a handful of cheap fundamentals via yfinance's existing Ticker.info (P/E, market cap, sector) -- explicitly did NOT add FRED/Polymarket/StockTwits/Reddit (real new data-source dependencies TradingAgents uses that would be real scope creep here). The prompt explicitly frames the ticker as one a mechanical system already flagged, asking for a second opinion, not a blind scan.

    No changes needed to swingtrade/config.py -- reuses the active config's existing ATR/stop fields with strategy="llm_agent" substituted, purely for schema-tagging. Buy_Price = today's Close (same convention as week52_high), Sell_Price/Stop_Loss via the same ATR-multiple formula every strategy uses, so it flows through settle_trade()/the existing Trade_Outcomes grading pipeline completely unchanged -- settle_trades.py needed ZERO changes, already fully strategy-agnostic. Deliberately NEVER wired into swingtrade/backtest.py, optimize.py, or benchmark_random_entry.py -- no elif branch for "llm_agent" anywhere in that dispatch chain, and neither CLI's --strategy choices include it. Verified directly: both tools reject --strategy llm_agent at the argparse level (invalid choice, immediate usage error) before even reaching the backtest code; separately confirmed swingtrade.run_backtest() itself raises a clear ValueError for strategy="llm_agent" when called with real (non-empty) ticker data, the same guardrail every other unsupported strategy string hits.

    Buy decisions tagged tier="actionable", Hold tagged tier="research" (same "accumulate real outcome data without being tradeable" pattern as every mechanical research tier), Avoid not logged at all -- via the existing storage.log_trade_signals() (already strategy-aware after this session's Trade_Signals/Trade_Outcomes schema migration, item 29). **Never passed to swingtrade.allocate_capital() -- no cash-pool input, no capital-allocation pass, full stop**, stated plainly in the tab's own UI copy regardless of how confident a call looks.

    Candidate pre-filter (cost/rate-limit control): the tab does NOT independently scan the watchlist -- candidates are tickers where at least one of v19/v27/v28 scored above Ignore today (reusing render_secondary_section()'s now-returned results_df, a small necessary signature change from () -> None to () -> pd.DataFrame), capped at MAX_LLM_CANDIDATES=10 by highest single mechanical Trade_Score. On days nothing mechanical fires (the current common case), the tab has nothing to evaluate and says so plainly -- an honest empty result, not a bug, matching every other empty-scan case this session.

    Dashboard restructured into st.tabs(["Mechanical Strategies", "LLM Agent (experimental)"]) -- tab 1 is every pre-existing section, re-indented under `with tab1:` but otherwise byte-for-byte unchanged (verified: AppTest before/after this change produced identical metric label lists -- 7 primary + 4x2 secondary = 15 -- and identical number_input labels). Tab 2 has an unmissable warning explaining the prospective-only validation constraint, the pre-filtered candidate list with each ticker's decision/confidence/rationale (expander-per-ticker, mirroring the existing AI Context section's UI), and a live validation-progress counter (settled llm_agent trade count, days since the first one, running win rate -- queried directly from Trade_Outcomes) so the "needs real time, not a quick backtest" constraint is visible in the UI itself. Proposed trust floor before drawing ANY conclusion: at least 20-30 settled trades AND at least 4-6 weeks of real elapsed time -- roughly double optimize.py's own MIN_TRADES_FOR_SCORE=15 floor, since prospective/LLM-noise data deserves a higher bar than a large backtested sample, not a lower one.

    Verified: llm_agent._parse_response() unit-tested against 9 hand-constructed cases (valid response, missing/invalid decision, out-of-range confidence both directions, non-numeric confidence, missing/empty rationale, malformed JSON, non-dict JSON, boundary confidence 0/100 inclusive) -- all passed exactly as predicted. evaluate_ticker() integration-tested with a mocked google.genai.Client (patched, not real API calls -- no GEMINI_API_KEY configured in this dev environment, same situation ai_context.py was originally built under and never contradicted since): confirmed a valid response parses correctly end-to-end AND that the prompt sent to the model actually includes the ticker and mechanical scores (not just that parsing works in isolation); confirmed an API exception degrades to None without raising; confirmed a non-JSON free-text response and an empty response both degrade to None. Gemini wins on every axis that matters here, so it correctly stays PRIMARY; Groq becomes the FALLBACK, tried only if Gemini is unavailable or its call fails/returns an unusable response. Groq is called via the standard `openai` SDK (already installed in this environment, now added to requirements.txt) pointed at Groq's OpenAI-compatible endpoint (https://api.groq.com/openai/v1) -- no Groq-specific SDK needed. is_available() now reflects EITHER provider being configured (GEMINI_API_KEY or GROQ_API_KEY, either alone is enough); the dashboard's "unavailable" caption updated to mention both keys and which role each plays.

    notifications.py: notify()/notify_with_file() gained an optional webhook_url: str | None = None parameter -- given, it's used directly; None (the default, and every existing daily_run.py call site) falls back to the shared DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL exactly as before. This one parameter satisfies both the unchanged existing caller and the new per-strategy caller with no separate fallback logic -- the "fall back to shared" behavior falls out of the existing default-parameter pattern. New get_strategy_webhook_url(strategy: str) -> str | None looks up DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL_{strategy.upper()} (e.g. "breakout_retest" -> DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL_BREAKOUT_RETEST), derived directly from config.strategy rather than any human-readable display label, so it can't drift from the actual strategy tag. Returns None if unset -- deliberately no fallback logic inside the helper itself; callers pass its result straight to notify(webhook_url=...), and notify()'s own default-parameter fallback handles "use shared webhook instead" once, in one place.

    ingest.py: new _build_signal_notification(label, results_df) -> str | None builds a short message listing that strategy's Strong Buy/Buy tickers (signal, Trade_Score, Buy_Price), sorted by Trade_Score descending; returns None when there are none, so the caller skips notifying entirely on quiet days, matching the confirmed cadence. Wired in once for the primary (v19) path (right after its existing "Logged N actionable..." print) and once inside run_secondary_strategy() for each of v27/v28 -- each call passes notifications.get_strategy_webhook_url(config.strategy) as the target, so v19 posts to DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL_BREAKOUT (or the shared fallback), v27 to DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL_BREAKOUT_RETEST, v28 to DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL_WEEK52_HIGH. A notification failure can't fail the scan -- notify() already degrades to False/a printed warning rather than raising (unchanged, pre-existing behavior), so no new try/except was needed at the call sites.

    .github/workflows/daily_run.yml: added three new optional secrets to the existing env: block (DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL_BREAKOUT, DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL_BREAKOUT_RETEST, DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL_WEEK52_HIGH), same pattern as the existing DISCORD_WEBHOOK_URL. 33. [DONE] LLM Agent tab: input richness -- explicit news sentiment + macro/market-wide context. Follow-up to items 30/32: user asked how much further the LLM side could go given TauricResearch/TradingAgents (multi-agent debate architecture) as inspiration, specifically calling out richer geopolitical/news sentiment input as the appealing near-term piece, with a genuine multi-agent pipeline as an explicit longer-term goal.     Also addressed a second idea floated alongside this: using multiple free LLM API keys "simultaneously" to fight cost. Checked the actual numbers before building anything -- today's volume is MAX_LLM_CANDIDATES=10 calls/day against Gemini's 1,500/day free limit, and this item adds zero new LLM calls (only more context text in the same single prompt), so multi-key load-balancing would add real complexity (differing response formats, reliability quirks, rotation logic) to solve a limit that's nowhere close to being hit.     Original plan called for GDELT's free DOC 2.0 API as the geopolitical-headline source (queried live during implementation, per this session's "verify before building" discipline) -- it returned HTTP 429 rate-limit errors consistently, minutes apart, on minimal queries, most likely shared-IP throttling on this sandbox's network egress rather than anything fixable by retrying.
